      His Holiness Bir Krishna Goswami· 2013-05-25· English
    • RGS SB 11-08-07-13 Hindi - Twenty-Four Guru-19
      His Holiness Radha Govinda Swami· English
    Personal Guidance
    Uncontrolled senses turn the mere sight of an alluring form into a fire — like the moth, the lusty mind rushes blindly into the darkness of material existence.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice the first second of attraction today — before words form, redirect the gaze and chant Kṛṣṇa's name as the cut-off.
    • Refuse to feed the loop: cull one specific input (a feed, a show, a chat) that you know functions as the moth's flame for you.
    • Speak to one trusted devotee this week about a viṣaya you struggle with, exposing it to light rather than letting it work in private.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which specific 'fire' do I keep circling — knowing it burns me — and what story do I tell myself to justify one more pass?
    • Where am I trusting my own ajita-indriya senses to handle exposure that the verse plainly says will capture me?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Audit your phone tonight — identify the one app or account that functions as your personal flame, and either delete it or hand the password to a friend for a fixed period.
    • Before a party, class trip, or date, fix in advance one line you will not cross and one devotee you will text if the situation tilts — pre-commit rather than negotiate in the moment.
    • Stop calling it 'just curiosity' when you re-open the same tab or DM thread; name it as a moth-circle and bring it up the next time you speak with a senior devotee.
    householder
    • Look honestly at the 'forms' your household chases — a lifestyle, a status, a body image — and name one specific way the family is currently flying at that flame.
    • Protect the marriage by guarding the eyes and ears in workplaces and screens; install filters, end private one-on-ones that have started to feel charged, and tell your spouse what you did and why.
    • Build the evening into a sense-cooling routine — kīrtana, reading, lights down, no late-night solo scrolling — so that the day does not end with the senses ungoverned.
    senior
    • Do not assume age has burned out the moth's instinct — examine which subtler viṣayas (respect, nostalgia, food, comfort) now play the role lust once did, and confess this honestly to Kṛṣṇa.
    • When mentoring younger devotees, share your actual scorch marks from the flame, not a sanitized version, so they learn the verse from a witness rather than a theorist.
    • Use the loosening of bodily life as the long-promised chance to withdraw the senses from their objects — fewer screens, simpler food, more japa — and let mortality finally do the work of vairāgya.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading the verse as anti-woman rather than as a diagnosis of one's own uncontrolled senses, missing that it applies equally to every form of viṣaya.
    • Treating disgust or moralistic suppression as vairāgya, when the verse calls for sober recognition of māyā and refuge in Kṛṣṇa, not white-knuckled aversion.
    • Assuming intellectual understanding of the moth-and-flame image is itself protection — the verse is precisely about how knowing does not stop the falling.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Where in modern life does the 'enticing form' show up for each of us — and do we actually agree on which forms are dangerous, or do we excuse our own?
    • Prabhupāda gives five creatures — moth, bee, elephant, deer, fish — as gurus of detachment; which one most exposes your particular weakness, and why that one?
    • Is the problem the form itself, the senses, or the mind that gets captured — and how does locating the problem change what we actually do tomorrow morning?
    Role Play

    Two students share a flat. One has just deleted a social media app after recognizing it as their moth-flame; the other says that is extreme, that mature devotees can engage anything without being captured, and offers to show them 'just one funny clip.' Play the conversation — first as it would actually go, then again with the first student responding from the standing of this verse without becoming preachy.

    Objections & Responses
    • This verse sounds repressive and unfair to women — it blames the form rather than the man's own choices.
      Read again: the verse names ajita-indriyaḥ, the one who has failed to control his senses, as the falling party. The form is described as deva-māyā, the Lord's illusory energy at work — the diagnosis is of the perceiver's condition, not a verdict on women. The same logic applies to every sense object; rūpa is just the example given here, with the other four creatures covering sound, touch, taste, and smell.
    • Modern psychology says suppression backfires; healthy integration of desire is the mature path, not fearing it like a moth.
      The verse is not prescribing suppression but sober recognition that the senses, untrained, are not neutral instruments — they are already pulled. Bhakti is not denial of desire but its redirection to Kṛṣṇa through sādhana; the moth analogy is simply honest about what happens when an untrained jīva insists it can fly close to the flame and integrate it.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.8

    ↗
    योषिद्धिरण्याभरणाम्बरादि- द्रव्येषु मायारचितेषु मूढ: । प्रलोभितात्मा ह्युपभोगबुद्ध्या पतङ्गवन्नश्यति नष्टद‍ृष्टि: ॥ ८ ॥
    yoṣid-dhiraṇyābharaṇāmbarādi-
    [yoshid-dhiranyaabharanaambaraadi-]
    dravyeṣu māyā-raciteṣu mūḍhaḥ
    [dravyeshu maayaa-rachiteshu moodhah]
    pralobhitātmā hy upabhoga-buddhyā
    [pralobhitaatmaa hy upabhoga-buddhyaa]
    pataṅga-van naśyati naṣṭa-dṛṣṭiḥ
    [patanga-van nashyati nashta-drishtih]
    Word-for-word

    whose intelligence.
    A foolish person with no intelligent discrimination is immediately aroused at the sight of a lusty woman beautifully decorated with golden ornaments, fine clothing and other cosmetic features. Being eager for sense gratification, such a fool loses all intelligence and is destroyed just like the moth who rushes into the blazing fire.
    Purport

    Actually, women have the power to attract men through all of the material senses. Men become lusty by seeing the body of a woman, by smelling her fragrance, by hearing her voice, by tasting her lips and by touching her body. However, the foolish relationship based on material sex attraction begins by seeing, and thus rūpa, or form, is very prominent in the process of ruining one’s intelligence. This fact has been exploited in modern times by huge pornography industries, which prey on unfortunate men and women. The example of the foolish moth rushing into the fire and destroying itself is most appropriate in this regard, for one who becomes addicted to the momentary pleasure of sex indulgence certainly loses his power to understand the spiritual reality behind dull matter.

    A lusty person becomes blind and foolish through sex indulgence, and his soul is lost in the fire of sense gratification. This whole disaster can be avoided by taking seriously to the process of chanting the holy names of the Lord: Hare Kṛṣṇa, Hare Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa Kṛṣṇa, Hare Hare/ Hare Rāma, Hare Rāma, Rāma Rāma, Hare Hare. Caitanya Mahāprabhu and His empowered representatives such as Śrīla Prabhupāda have created a movement to save people from the dungeon of material life, and we should all seriously take advantage of this opportunity.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    A foolish person, lured by a woman, gold, ornaments or fine clothing made by māyā, with a desire for enjoyment, loses his vision and is destroyed, just like the moth which rushes into a fire. Though the five sense objects are involved in being attracted to a woman, among all enjoyable objects, first the sense of sight falls victim to women or other desired objects. Thus form is the chief sense object.
    Related lectures
    • BKG SB 11-08-08 - Overcoming Sex Desire - 2013-05-26 New Talavan
      His Holiness Bir Krishna Goswami· 2013-05-26· English
    • JAS SB 11-08-08 - The Lesson From The Moth - 2018-04-01 ISKCON Chowpatty
      His Holiness Jayadvaita Swami· 2018-04-01· English
    Personal Guidance
    Like a moth rushing the flame, the lusty fool drawn by form and ornament loses his discrimination and is destroyed — guard the eye, chant the holy names.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice the first glance today — the moment rūpa hooks the mind through a body, an image, or a screen — and withdraw the senses before a second look fuses you to it.
    • Replace the pornographic and lust-provoking inputs in your day (feeds, shows, ads) with audible chanting of Hare Kṛṣṇa, so the same senses that were burning fuel the soul instead.
    • Refuse the modern myth that sexual indulgence is harmless adult freedom — name it for what the verse calls it: the moth's last flight into the fire.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my life am I the moth — circling something I know will destroy my intelligence, telling myself one more pass is safe?
    • Which sense gate (seeing, hearing, smelling, tasting, touching) most reliably loots my discrimination, and what sādhana have I been avoiding that would close it?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Audit your phone tonight — delete or mute the two accounts you follow mainly because the person is attractive; the moth doesn't have to land in the fire, it dies from circling it.
    • Pick a specific high-temptation slot in your week (a party, a late-night scroll, a particular group chat) and pre-decide a chanting or association alternative before the moment arrives, not during it.
    • Talk honestly with one trusted devotee friend about what your eyes have been doing this week — secrecy is the fuel the verse warns about; daylight starves it.
    householder
    • Protect the marriage by treating outside lusty inputs — coworker flirtation, suggestive shows watched 'together,' algorithmic feeds — as the moth's flame, not as harmless background, and remove them from the home today.
    • Model guarded senses in front of your children: what you watch, comment on, and laugh at trains their dṛṣṭi more than any lecture on chastity ever will.
    • Use a portion of household income deliberately for sādhu-saṅga, devotional books, and temple service — redirecting the budget that the world expects you to spend on indulgence into the chanting culture the purport calls the rescue.
    senior
    • Recognize that the moth's pull does not retire with the body — subtler forms (nostalgic fantasies, online wandering, prestige-seeking) still scorch the intelligence; chant aloud to keep them named and small.
    • Mentor a younger person honestly about the costs you paid for indulgences your generation called normal, so they hear the verse from a witness rather than a stranger.
    • Loosen your grip on ornaments — possessions, photographs, status objects you have decorated your life with — by giving at least one away each week to a devotee or service, training the dṛṣṭi the verse asks for before the body forces the lesson.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading the verse as anti-woman rather than as a diagnosis of one's own pralobhita-ātmā — the mind seduced by māyā-racita forms, of which a decorated body is only the standard example.
    • Treating lust as a private weakness to white-knuckle through, instead of a fire that requires the active medicine of chanting and association the purport prescribes.
    • Congratulating oneself on outward celibacy while feeding the eyes and imagination all day — the moth doesn't have to land in the flame to already be circling it.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one concrete input you consume daily that you suspect is the 'golden ornament' for you — and whether you are willing, in this room, to commit to cutting it.
    • The purport names modern pornography industries explicitly. Where do group members disagree about how strict a Gauḍīya practitioner should be with mainstream films, music, and social media?
    • Prabhupāda says women too are preyed upon by these industries — how does this verse read when the lusty seer and the decorated object are not split along gender lines our culture assumes?
    Role Play

    A college-age student tells their older friend: 'I scroll reels for an hour every night before bed — it's just relaxation, everyone does it, and I still chant my rounds in the morning.' One student plays the scroller, defending the habit; another plays the friend who has read SB 11.8.8 and must respond without moralizing, using the moth-and-flame image and the chanting remedy from the purport. Switch roles after five minutes.

    Objections & Responses
    • This verse is patriarchal — it blames women for men's lust and treats female beauty as a trap.
      The verse is diagnosing the deluded seer, not the seen. The 'fault' named is naṣṭa-dṛṣṭiḥ — lost vision — in the one who looks with upabhoga-buddhi. Prabhupāda himself extends the same warning to women preyed upon by the industries of lust. The teaching is symmetrical: any soul that takes a māyā-decorated form as the object of enjoyment burns.
    • Sexual attraction is biology — calling it the moth's death is religious exaggeration in an adult, consenting world.
      Biology is exactly the point — the moth's pull to the flame is also biology, and it still kills him. The verse is not denying the force; it is naming where the force leads when intelligence (dṛṣṭi) is gone. A Gauḍīya teacher does not ask you to feel nothing, but to keep your discrimination intact and to take shelter of the holy name, which is the only thing strong enough to redirect that fire toward Kṛṣṇa.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.9

    ↗
    स्तोकं स्तोकं ग्रसेद् ग्रासं देहो वर्तेत यावता । गृहानहिंसन्नातिष्ठेद् वृत्तिं माधुकरीं मुनि: ॥ ९ ॥
    stokaṁ stokaṁ grased grāsaṁ

    stokam stokam — always, a little bit graset — one should eat grāsam — food dehaḥ — the material body varteta — that it may live yāvatā — with that much gṛhān — the householders ahiṁsan — not harassing ātiṣṭhet — one should practice vṛttim — the occupation mādhu-karīm — of the honeybee muniḥ — a saintly person.

    muniḥ
    a saintly person.
    A saintly person should accept only enough food to keep his body and soul together. He should go from door to door accepting just a little bit of food from each family. Thus he should practice the occupation of the honeybee.
    Purport

    Sometimes a honeybee is attracted by the extraordinary aroma of a particular lotus flower and lingers there, neglecting his usual activity of flying from flower to flower. Unfortunately, at sunset the lotus flower closes, and thus the infatuated honeybee is trapped. Similarly, a sannyāsī or brahmacārī may discover that excellent foodstuff is available at a particular house, and therefore, instead of wandering from place to place, he may become a veritable resident of such a well-fed household. Thus he will become bewildered by the illusion of family life and fall down from the platform of renunciation. Also, if a mendicant takes unfair advantage of the Vedic custom of charity and takes too often from a single family, the resentment he causes will disturb the social order. An ideal sage should, like the honeybee, travel from place to place, but he must be careful not to become a chubby bee by going to many houses and eating sumptuously at each house. According to Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura, such a chubby bee will undoubtedly become entangled in the network of māyā. No one should be overly attached to the gratification of his tongue, because this will lead to an overstuffed belly and then uncontrollable lust. In conclusion, one should not endeavor very much for material sense gratification but should instead endeavor strenuously to spread the glories of the Supreme Lord, Kṛṣṇa. This is the proper use of human energy.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    A saintly person should accept only enough food to keep his body alive. He should go from door to door accepting just a little bit of food from each family. Thus he should practice the occupation of the honeybee. He learns from the bee in two verses. Just as the bee stays in one lotus because of greed for its special fragrance, and becomes trapped inside when the petals close at night, the sage, taking shelter of one house because of greed for its good qualities, becomes trapped there by that illusion. Therefore, one should accept a little from each house and eat only to keep the body alive. One commits violence against the householders by taking too much or too little.

      His Holiness Jayadvaita Swami· 2018-04-03· English
    Personal Guidance
    Eat only what keeps body and soul together, gather it lightly from many sources like a honeybee, and never let a comfortable host become your trap.
    Practical Applications
    • Decide before each meal the smallest portion that will actually sustain your service, then stop there even if more is offered.
    • Notice the household, restaurant, or kitchen whose comfort is quietly anchoring you, and rotate away before its sweetness becomes a cage.
    • Refuse to repeatedly draw from a single generous source until your presence starts to strain the giver.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which "lotus flower" of comfortable provision am I lingering in past sunset, and what renunciation is it slowly dissolving in me?
    • Where am I using the language of accepting prasāda or hospitality to disguise an attachment to my tongue and belly?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Pre-commit to a fixed plate size in the hostel mess or canteen and walk away when it is finished, even when friends are still going back for more.
    • Notice the one home, hangout, or food-delivery app that has become your default "lotus flower" and deliberately diversify so no single comfort owns your evenings.
    • Refuse to keep mooching repeatedly off the same generous friend or relative; rotate, contribute, and protect the relationship from quiet resentment.
    householder
    • Set a household norm that meals are sized to sustain service the next day, not to crown the day's pleasures, and serve children and guests from that same standard.
    • Audit which restaurants, sweet shops, or kitchens your family has become emotionally settled into, and intentionally loosen that grip before it shapes your monthly budget and your tongue.
    • When hosting traveling devotees or relatives, feed them well but lovingly help them remain mādhukarī — do not become the trap that ends their wandering and their renunciation.
    senior
    • Use this stage to actively shrink your intake — fewer meals, simpler foods, smaller portions — as a tangible rehearsal for leaving the body without the tongue still grasping.
    • Identify the one home, child's house, or comfort routine you have started treating as a permanent lotus flower, and consciously practice non-residence there in heart even while bodily present.
    • Mentor younger devotees by showing, not just telling, how a senior honeybee moves lightly — eating little, depending on no single source, and giving more than they take wherever they go.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Treating the honeybee principle as license to graze indulgently from many sources instead of taking a little from each.
    • Mistaking attachment to one well-stocked kitchen for legitimate community life and refusing to see the entanglement forming.
    • Externally moving from place to place while internally rehearsing the next sumptuous meal, so the body wanders but the tongue stays settled.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a specific "lotus flower" in your life — a household, a cuisine, a host — that you suspect is closing around you, and let the group press on whether it is service or sense-gratification.
    • Debate honestly: in a modern city, is the honeybee-vṛtti a literal instruction for renunciants or a transferable discipline for working devotees, and where does each reading break down?
    • Where in your community have you seen a sincere person quietly "fall down from the platform of renunciation" through the kitchen rather than through obvious sin — and what early signs did everyone miss?
    Role Play

    One student plays a traveling brahmacārī who has been staying ten days at a devotee family's home because the prasāda is exceptional and the hostess insists; another plays the householder, warmly pressing him to stay longer; a third plays a senior Vaiṣṇava who has come to gently confront the situation in light of SB 11.8.9 — work through the conversation until each person can name what mādhukarī-vṛtti would actually look like here.

    Objections & Responses
    • This is a renunciate's instruction — I'm a householder with a job and a family, so the honeybee verse has nothing to say to me.
      The outer practice is for the muni, but the inner principle — accept only what sustains service, refuse to let any single comfort domesticate you, and stay vigilant about the tongue and belly — is exactly what protects a householder from sliding into mere viṣaya under the cover of family duty.
    • If a host is happy to feed me sumptuously every day, my refusing seems ungrateful and even insulting to their devotion.
      Prabhupāda is blunt that the chubby bee gets caught in māyā and that overuse of one family's charity disturbs the social order; real gratitude is to honor their devotion without exploiting it, take a little, bless them, and move on so that both renunciation and the relationship stay healthy.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.10

    ↗
    अणुभ्यश्च महद्भ्यश्च शास्त्रेभ्य: कुशलो नर: । सर्वत: सारमादद्यात् पुष्पेभ्य इव षट्पद: ॥ १० ॥
    aṇubhyaś ca mahadbhyaś ca
    [anubhyash cha mahadbhyash cha]
    śāstrebhyaḥ kuśalo naraḥ
    [shaastrebhyah kushalo narah]
    sarvataḥ sāram ādadyāt
    [sarvatah saaram aadadyaat]
    puṣpebhya iva ṣaṭpadaḥ
    [pushpebhya iva shatpadah]
    Word-for-word
    aṇubhyaḥ — from the smallest ca — and mahadbhyaḥ — from the greatest ca — also śāstrebhyaḥ — from religious scriptures kuśalaḥ — intelligent naraḥ — a man sarvataḥ — from all sāram — the essence ādadyāt — should take puṣpebhyaḥ — from the flowers iva — like ṣaṭpadaḥ — the honeybee.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    like
    ṣaṭpadaḥ
    the honeybee.
    Just as the honeybee takes nectar from all flowers, big and small, an intelligent human being should take the essence from all religious scriptures.
    Purport

    In human society the original knowledge is called Veda, and the essential part of veda, or knowledge, is Kṛṣṇa consciousness. As stated in Bhagavad-gītā (15.15) , vedaiś ca sarvair aham eva vedyaḥ. From the honeybee, an intelligent human being should learn to take the essence, or nectar, of all knowledge. A honeybee does not waste its time trying to carry away an entire bush or garden, but rather takes the essential nectar. We may thus study the difference between the honeybee and the ass, who carries heavy loads. Education does not mean to become an intellectual ass carrying heavy loads of useless knowledge; rather, one should accept the essential knowledge that leads to an eternal life of bliss and understanding.

    At the present time people generally have a sectarian concept of religion, and yet there is no scientific understanding of the Absolute Truth. Such complacent, dogmatic, sectarian religionists can certainly learn something from the example of the honeybee given in this verse.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Just as the honeybee takes nectar from all flowers, big and small, an intelligent human being should take the essence from all religious scriptures. He learns from the bee to take the essence of dharma from scriptures.
    Related lectures
    • Prabha Vishnu Pr SB 11-08-10 - 2009-05-05 ISKCON Melbourne
      His Grace Pariksit Prabhu· 2009-05-05· English
    • JAS SB 11-08-10 - Essence Seeker like Honeybee - 2018-04-04 ISKCON Chowpatty
      His Holiness Jayadvaita Swami· 2018-04-04· English
    Personal Guidance
    Like the honeybee gathering nectar from every flower, an intelligent student extracts the Kṛṣṇa-conscious essence from all scriptures rather than hoarding loads of information.
    Practical Applications
    • When you read a śāstra today, write down one sentence of essence you can act on, not three pages of notes you will never revisit.
    • Refuse to weigh yourself down with sectarian quarrels about which book is highest; ask instead what each text reveals about Kṛṣṇa.
    • Notice when you are reading to accumulate prestige rather than nectar, and stop to chant or remember Kṛṣṇa instead.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my study life am I behaving like the ass with heavy loads rather than the honeybee taking nectar?
    • What sectarian or intellectual bush have I been clinging to that is keeping me from the essence the verse points toward?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before your next exam, ask which facts are nectar for your life and which are loads you will dump the day after the test, and study accordingly.
    • When friends argue online about whose religion or ideology is best, practice being the honeybee in the comments — take any truth, leave the quarrel.
    • Pick one verse from your reading this week and actually live it for a day instead of scrolling through ten more reels of spiritual content.
    householder
    • Choose one short scriptural passage to read aloud with your spouse or children at dinner and distill it into one doable family resolution.
    • Audit the books, podcasts, and courses you consume for work and self-improvement; cut the ones that add weight but no nectar for your sādhana.
    • When making a family decision, ask which option a kuśala devotee would extract as essence, rather than defaulting to what the neighbors or relatives approve.
    senior
    • Of all the books on your shelf, mark the three whose essence you actually want to pass on, and begin reading those with a younger devotee.
    • Let go of the urge to be seen as widely read; speak less of what you have studied and more of the one nectar — remembrance of Kṛṣṇa — you have tasted.
    • When mentoring, model the honeybee: receive the questioner's tradition or background respectfully, then point gently to Kṛṣṇa as the essence they are already seeking.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Treating the honeybee analogy as license for shallow eclecticism that picks pleasing bits while ignoring Kṛṣṇa as the essence of all Veda.
    • Confusing volume of reading with realization, and mistaking a full bookshelf for a softened heart.
    • Using the verse to dismiss careful study of a single ācārya's books, when honeybee-discrimination presupposes serious work, not laziness.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one scripture or book outside our line that you have learned from; what nectar did you take, and what did you leave on the bush?
    • Where does the line fall for you between honest essence-taking and a self-flattering pick-and-choose religiosity?
    • Tell the group about a time you carried heavy intellectual loads that did not bring you closer to Kṛṣṇa; what would the honeybee have done differently?
    Role Play

    A college friend hands you three books from three different traditions and says, "You Vaiṣṇavas are narrow; read these and prove you are open-minded." Two students play it out: one as the friend pressing the challenge, one as the student applying SB 11.8.10 honestly, neither dismissing the books nor surrendering the Gauḍīya conclusion.

    Objections & Responses
    • If every scripture has nectar, why insist that Kṛṣṇa consciousness is the essence? Is that not just your own sectarianism dressed up as honeybee wisdom?
      Prabhupāda's purport is explicit: vedaiś ca sarvair aham eva vedyaḥ — Kṛṣṇa is what the Vedas are meant to reveal. The honeybee does not pretend all parts of the flower are nectar; it discriminates. Naming Kṛṣṇa as the essence is the discrimination, not the abandonment of it.
    • This verse sounds like a green light for spiritual cherry-picking — a little Gītā, a little Buddha, a little Stoicism — whatever suits me.
      The verse praises the kuśala, the skilled person, not the lazy consumer. A honeybee works hard, flies far, and serves the hive. Essence-taking under a bona fide ācārya is not curating a personal smoothie; it is being trained to recognize nectar from sap.
    📝 My note

    saṅgrahī
    a collector.
    A saintly person should not think, “This food I will keep to eat tonight and this other food I can save for tomorrow.” In other words, a saintly person should not store foodstuffs acquired by begging. Rather, he should use his own hands as his plate and eat whatever fits on them. His only storage container should be his belly, and whatever conveniently fits into his belly should be his stock of food. Thus one should not imitate the greedy honeybee who eagerly collects more and more honey.
    Purport

    There are two types of honeybees: those who collect nectar from the flowers and those who actually manufacture honey in the beehive. This verse refers to the second class. The greedy honeybee eventually collects so much honey that he is trapped in his hive; and in the same way, a materialistic person becomes trapped under the burden of unnecessary material accumulation. One who is interested in making spiritual progress should avoid such a situation; however, Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura points out that for the purpose of spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness one may accumulate an unlimited amount of material opulence. This is called yukta-vairāgya, or using everything in the service of Kṛṣṇa. A saintly person who is unable to work in Lord Caitanya’s mission should practice austerities and collect only what he can hold in his hands and belly. However, one who has given his life to Kṛṣṇa may collect unlimitedly on Lord Kṛṣṇa’s behalf. In fact, without acquiring material opulence, how is it possible to spread the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement all over the world? But if one tries to personally enjoy the funds or facilities acquired for the missionary activities of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, he commits the greatest offense. Therefore, even in the name of Lord Kṛṣṇa, one should collect only what he can immediately engage in practical devotional service; otherwise one will fall down to the platform of ordinary greediness.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The sage should not collect food, storing it for the evening or the next day, but rather should use his hands as his plate and his belly as his storage vessel. He should not collect and store food as the bee collects honey and stores it. He should not collect food thinking, “I will eat this tonight or tomorrow.” What to speak of thinking of food from the previous day, the previous month or previous full moon. One should not make plans for food one sees, and should also not accept invitations for the evening or next day, according to some authorities. With what vessel should one beg from house to house? One should use one’s hands as a vessel. Having obtained food where should one place it? One should put it in the belly. He has his belly as his storage vessel (amatraḥ).

      His Grace Pariksit Prabhu· 2009-05-06· English
    • JAS SB 11-08-11 - Use Lakshmi in Krishna%27s Service - 2018-04-05 ISKCON Chowpatty
      His Holiness Jayadvaita Swami· 2018-04-05· English
    Personal Guidance
    Take only what fits in your hand and belly today; the greedy honeybee, hoarding more than it can use, gets trapped in its own hive.
    Practical Applications
    • Audit one category of accumulation today — clothes, gadgets, pantry, savings beyond need — and give away or simplify what exceeds present use.
    • Before any new purchase or stockpiling, ask aloud: is this for Kṛṣṇa's service (yukta-vairāgya) or for my private enjoyment?
    • Eat one meal today with the bhikṣu's attitude — receive only what is given, store nothing for tomorrow, thank Kṛṣṇa it was enough.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my life am I manufacturing honey I will never eat — accumulating for a future enjoyer who never quite arrives?
    • If I had to justify each thing I store as immediately engageable in devotional service, what would I have to release?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Open your phone's storage, photo library, or saved-items folder and delete what you have been hoarding 'just in case' — notice the honeybee impulse in digital form.
    • Before the next sale, drop, or limited edition, wait twenty-four hours and ask whether it serves your sādhana or only your peer image.
    • Try one day this week eating only what is offered or already in the kitchen — no ordering in, no stocking up — and watch what arises when the hoarding option is removed.
    householder
    • Sit with your spouse and review one savings or shopping category honestly — what portion serves the family's dharma and Kṛṣṇa's service, what portion is honey you will never eat?
    • When tempted to upgrade house, car, or lifestyle beyond the family's actual need, name the upgrade aloud as honeybee accumulation and choose whether to proceed with eyes open.
    • Teach your children the difference between providing and hoarding by letting them see you give away surplus this week — clothes outgrown, books finished, food extra.
    senior
    • Begin actively distributing now what you had planned to leave in a will — books, tools, ornaments, savings — so the giving is conscious, not posthumous.
    • Identify one attachment you have quietly grown around in late life (a room, a routine, a reserve fund) and loosen your grip on it this month as practice for the final loosening.
    • Mentor a younger devotee on the honeybee trap you have personally fallen into and climbed out of — your specific story, not a general warning.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Using yukta-vairāgya as a cover story — collecting opulence supposedly for Kṛṣṇa while quietly enjoying it personally, which the purport calls the greatest offense.
    • Imitating the literal renunciation of a bhikṣu while still a householder, neglecting dependents who legitimately rely on your provision.
    • Confusing minimalism as a lifestyle aesthetic with the bhikṣu's actual dependence on Kṛṣṇa — keeping the look of austerity while the heart still hoards.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Where is the honest line for you between prudent provision for your āśrama and the honeybee's trapped hoarding — and how did you arrive at it?
    • Prabhupāda says one engaged in Lord Caitanya's mission may collect unlimitedly; one who cannot must take only handful-and-belly. Which side are you actually on, and how would you know if you were deceiving yourself?
    • Share one possession or savings bucket you feel defensive about discussing — what does that defensiveness reveal?
    Role Play

    One student plays a devotee who has been raising funds for a temple project and has begun keeping a personal reserve 'for emergencies'; the other plays a senior godbrother who has noticed. Run the conversation where the godbrother gently raises the honeybee verse and the fundraiser must respond honestly — not theatrically renounce, not defensively justify.

    Objections & Responses
    • This is irresponsible in a modern economy — without savings, retirement accounts, and insurance, you become a burden on family or the state.
      The verse addresses the saintly mendicant, not the householder, and Prabhupāda explicitly preserves yukta-vairāgya — using everything in Kṛṣṇa's service. A householder provides responsibly for dependents and for preaching; what the verse forbids in every āśrama is accumulation for private enjoyment, the inner posture of the honeybee storing honey it cannot eat.
    • Telling preachers they can collect unlimitedly while ordinary devotees must live hand-to-mouth sounds like a loophole for institutional greed.
      Prabhupāda himself anticipated this and called personal enjoyment of mission funds the greatest offense. The test is not the size of the collection but whether each rupee can be immediately engaged in practical devotional service; the moment surplus is held back for private use, the same verse condemns the preacher as a honeybee.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.12

    ↗
    सायन्तनं श्वस्तनं वा न सङ्गृह्णीत भिक्षुक: । मक्षिका इव सङ्गृह्णन् सह तेन विनश्यति ॥ १२ ॥
    sāyantanaṁ śvastanaṁ vā

    na saṅgṛhṇīta bhikṣukaḥ

    makṣikā iva saṅgṛhṇan
    [makshikaa iva sangrihnan]
    saha tena vinaśyati

    A saintly mendicant should not even collect foodstuffs to eat later in the same day or the next day. If he disregards this injunction and like the honeybee collects more and more delicious foodstuffs, that which he has collected will indeed ruin him.
    Purport

    Bhramara refers to that honeybee who wanders about from flower to flower, and makṣikā is the honeybee who accumulates more and more honey in the beehive with great attachment. A saintly mendicant should be like the bhramara bee because if he imitates the makṣikā bee his spiritual consciousness will be ruined. This point is so important that it is repeated in this verse.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    A saintly mendicant should not collect foodstuffs to eat later in the same day or the next day. If he disregards this injunction and like the honeybee collects food, he will be destroyed along with the food. If he hoards food, what happens? That is explained in this verse.
    Related lectures
    • Prabha Vishnu Pr SB 11-08-12 - 2009-05-07 ISKCON Melbourne
      His Grace Pariksit Prabhu· 2009-05-07· English
    Personal Guidance
    Like the bhramara who sips and moves on, take only what today requires; the makṣikā habit of hoarding what is "yours" quietly ruins the renunciant within.
    Practical Applications
    • Identify one category where you stockpile "just in case" — food, books, clothes, even tabs — and take only what today truly needs.
    • Refuse the makṣikā logic of "a little more won't hurt"; treat each accumulation as a hidden attachment that must be named, not justified.
    • Before any purchase or saving move today, ask whether it is for sustenance or for storage, and let only sustenance pass.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my life have I quietly shifted from bhramara to makṣikā — gathering far beyond what sādhana actually requires?
    • What am I telling myself "belongs to me for later" that is in fact slowly ruining my taste for dependence on Kṛṣṇa?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Notice the makṣikā move in your phone — saved posts, hoarded screenshots, endless bookmarks — and clear what you will honestly never return to.
    • Refuse the campus reflex of stockpiling certifications, internships, and contacts "for later"; choose the one that serves sva-dharma now and release the rest.
    • When friends pressure you to overspend or overcommit "because it might be useful someday," name it as makṣikā logic and step out.
    householder
    • Audit one shelf, one folder, or one bank reserve this week and distinguish what genuinely protects the family from what is simply accumulation in disguise.
    • Before adding another stream of income or possession, ask whether it strengthens your capacity for bhakti or only thickens your dependence on storage.
    • Teach the children, by your own restraint at the table and the store, that "enough for today" is a sane and dignified standard, not deprivation.
    senior
    • Begin giving away today what you have been keeping "in case"; let your home visibly become lighter as your dependence on Kṛṣṇa becomes heavier.
    • Refuse the late-life makṣikā temptation to amass legacy — property, reputation, even seniority in the community — and quietly hand it on.
    • Mentor a younger devotee by showing, not lecturing, how a life can be arranged around "just enough for sādhana today" rather than around reserves.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Mistaking the verse as a ban on planning, then swinging into reckless improvidence rather than detached sufficiency.
    • Applying the injunction outwardly — owning little — while inwardly hoarding grievances, credits, and spiritual "merit."
    • Imagining this applies only to formal mendicants, exempting one's own household life from any examination of accumulation.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Where exactly does prudent provision end and makṣikā-style hoarding begin in our current stage of life — and who decides?
    • Share one thing you have stored "for tomorrow" that, examined honestly, you suspect you will never actually need.
    • If a saintly mendicant must not even keep tomorrow's meal, what does the spirit of this verse demand of those of us who are not mendicants?
    Role Play

    One student plays a sincere practitioner who has just been offered a large bulk discount on six months of groceries and supplies, citing prudence and family responsibility; another plays a senior devotee who has read SB 11.8.12 that morning. Work through the conversation until both can name where bhramara-bhāva ends and makṣikā-bhāva begins for this specific household.

    Objections & Responses
    • This is romantic medieval advice; in a modern economy without savings, insurance, and reserves, my family will simply suffer.
      The verse is spoken to the bhikṣuka, the renunciant, as his standard; for the householder it sets a direction, not a literal rule. The point is the inner posture — do I lean on accumulation or on Kṛṣṇa? Reasonable provision under varṇāśrama is not condemned here; makṣikā-style clinging, even to modest reserves, is.
    • Hoarding food is hardly my problem — I am not a bee or a beggar, so this verse has nothing to say to me.
      The honeybee is an image, not a limit. Wherever you collect "a little more" of anything — money, status, approval, even spiritual experiences — beyond what today's service requires, you are the makṣikā. The verse asks you to find where that pattern actually lives in your life.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.13

    ↗
    पदापि युवतीं भिक्षुर्न स्पृशेद् दारवीमपि । स्पृशन् करीव बध्येत करिण्या अङ्गसङ्गत: ॥ १३ ॥
    padāpi yuvatīṁ bhikṣur
    [padaapi yuvateem bhikshur]

    [sprishan kareeva badhyeta]

    padā — with the foot api — even yuvatīm — a young girl bhikṣuḥ — a saintly mendicant na — not spṛśet — should touch dāravīm — made of wood api — even spṛśan — touching karī — the elephant iva — like badhyeta — becomes captured kariṇyāḥ — of the she-elephant aṅga-saṅgataḥ — by contact with the body.

    by contact with the body.
    A saintly person should never touch a young girl. In fact, he should not even let his foot touch a wooden doll in the shape of a woman. By bodily contact with a woman he will surely be captured by illusion, just as the elephant is captured by the she-elephant due to his desire to touch her body.
    Purport

    Elephants are captured in the jungle in the following way. A large hole is dug and then covered over with grass, leaves, mud and so on. Then a she-elephant is exhibited in front of the male elephant, who chases after her with lusty desire, falls into the hole and is captured. The lesson to be learned from the elephant is that the desire to relish the touch sensation is certainly the cause of ruining one’s life. An intelligent person, noting the elephant’s great propensity to sport with the she-elephant, will take this excellent example to heart. Therefore, somehow or other one should avoid being cheated by allurement to the sensuous form of woman. One should not allow one’s mind to be lost in lusty dreams of sex pleasure. There are various types of sense gratification to be enjoyed between men and women, including speaking, contemplating, touching, sexual intercourse, etc., and all of these constitute the network of illusion by which one is helplessly bound like an animal. Somehow or other one should remain aloof from sense gratification in the form of sex pleasure; otherwise, there is no possibility of understanding the spiritual world.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    A saintly person should never touch a young girl even with his foot. In fact, he should not even let his foot touch a wooden doll in the shape of a woman. He will be captured by illusion, just as the elephant is captured by desire to touch the she-elephant’s body. Attachment to touch is a cause of destruction. This he learned from the elephant. This is explained in two verses. One should not touch a young woman even with one’s foot, and should not even touch a wooden form of a woman. The elephant, seeing the female elephant, falls into a hole covered with grass and is trapped.
    Related lectures
    • RGS SB 11-08-13-20 Hindi - Twenty-Four Guru-20
      His Holiness Radha Govinda Swami· English
    • 2011-01-12 SB 11-08-13 - Story Of Pingala - Krishnakrpa Pr ISKCON Gainesville
      ISKCON Gainesville· 2011-01-12· English
    Personal Guidance
    Sense-gratification through contact with the opposite sex binds the soul like the bull elephant lured into the pit — for the renunciant, even symbolic contact is too close.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today every small impulse to seek physical proximity or charged contact, and refuse the first move rather than negotiating with the tenth.
    • Set one concrete boundary around sight, speech, or touch that you have been quietly relaxing, and hold it for the rest of the day.
    • When the mind drifts into lusty rehearsal, immediately redirect it to Kṛṣṇa's name or form rather than arguing with the fantasy.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my life am I behaving like the elephant — chasing a small sensory pleasure that is steadily digging a pit beneath my sādhana?
    • Which 'wooden doll' — a harmless-looking proxy for desire — am I telling myself does not really count?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Decide in advance — not in the moment — what you will and will not do on dates, in DMs, and at parties, because the elephant never plans his fall.
    • Curate your feed today: unfollow the accounts that exist mainly to bait the touch-impulse the verse warns about, even the ones you tell yourself are 'just aesthetic'.
    • When a flirtation starts to feel charged, name it honestly to one trusted friend within twenty-four hours instead of letting it grow in private.
    householder
    • Audit the small 'wooden doll' habits — lingering chats, private messaging, suggestive media consumed alone — that you would not want your spouse to see, and end one of them today.
    • Treat your marriage vow as the householder's version of this verse: protect it not only in gross action but in speech, attention, and contemplation, beginning with how you speak about colleagues at home tonight.
    • Teach your children, by what you refuse as much as by what you say, that bodily contact carries real karmic weight and is not a casual currency.
    senior
    • At this stage, take the verse as permission to formally retire from arenas of subtle flirtation — tone, attention, charm — that you have carried by habit for decades.
    • Mentor a younger practitioner specifically on this teaching, sharing where you yourself were the elephant, so the lesson is transmitted from experience rather than theory.
    • Use the loosening grip of bodily desire as an opportunity to deepen vairāgya rather than to mourn it, redirecting freed attention into japa, study, and service.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading the verse as anti-woman moralism rather than as a precise diagnosis of how viṣaya hijacks the renunciant's mind.
    • Assuming the warning applies only to formal sannyāsīs while ignoring its force for any serious student cultivating vairāgya.
    • Congratulating oneself on avoiding the gross act while freely indulging the subtle forms — gazing, contemplating, conversing — that the purport explicitly names.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Where is the honest line between healthy interaction with the opposite sex in modern life and the 'bodily contact' the verse warns against — and who in the room disagrees?
    • Prabhupāda lists speaking, contemplating, and touching as parts of one network of illusion; which of these does our group most underestimate, and why?
    • If the elephant's ruin begins with a desire to touch, what is the earliest internal signal each of us could learn to catch before the pit opens?
    Role Play

    A brahmacārī student tells his senior that a female classmate has been asking to study one-on-one in her room because the library is closed; she is sincere, the project is genuine, and refusing feels rude. Two students play the brahmacārī and the senior, working through how to apply this verse without becoming either reckless or self-righteous.

    Objections & Responses
    • This is a medieval, fear-based view of women that has no place in a mature spiritual life — surely a self-aware practitioner can manage ordinary contact.
      The verse is not about women; it is about the practitioner's own mind, which Kṛṣṇa's devotee Uddhava is being warned is not as managed as he believes. The elephant also thought he was in control until the moment he fell. Gauḍīya teachers honor women as souls precisely by refusing to use them as objects of one's untested confidence.
    • If even a wooden doll is too dangerous, the instruction is impossible for anyone living in society — it can only mean something symbolic.
      The strictness is calibrated to the bhikṣu — the renunciant whose whole life is staked on undivided absorption in Kṛṣṇa. For the householder the same principle scales down to fidelity, guarded association, and disciplined senses; the teaching is not abolished, it is proportioned to one's āśrama.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.14

    ↗
    नाधिगच्छेत् स्त्रियं प्राज्ञ: कर्हिचिन्मृत्युमात्मन: । बलाधिकै: स हन्येत गजैरन्यैर्गजो यथा ॥ १४ ॥

    A man possessing intelligent discrimination should not under any circumstances try to exploit the beautiful form of a woman for his sense gratification. Just as an elephant trying to enjoy a she-elephant is killed by other bull elephants also enjoying her company, one trying to enjoy a lady’s company can at any moment be killed by her other lovers who are stronger than he.
    Purport

    Just as one is enchanted by the beautiful form of a woman, many other men will also be enchanted, and there is danger that they will be stronger than oneself and may even jealously kill one. Crimes of passion are extremely common. This is another disadvantage of material life.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    An intelligent man should not at any time exploit a woman who is death itself, because he will be killed by stronger lovers, just as an elephant trying to enjoy a she-elephant is killed by other stronger bull elephants One should not boldly approach a woman, while thinking, “I will enjoy her.” He will be killed by stronger lovers brought by her.
    Personal Guidance
    A discerning man does not try to exploit a woman's beauty for enjoyment — like a bull elephant, he can be destroyed by stronger rivals competing for her.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today the precise moment your eyes or imagination begin to exploit a woman's form, and withdraw the senses back to the breath and the holy name.
    • Refuse to enter — physically or digitally — any setting whose attraction depends on viṣaya-bhoga of the opposite sex, even when peers normalize it.
    • Speak to one trusted devotee this week about where lust is actually arising in your life, rather than managing it alone in your head.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my life am I treating a woman primarily as an object of enjoyment, and what unseen "stronger elephants" — rivals, consequences, karmic recoil — am I refusing to see?
    • What does "intelligent discrimination" (the prājña of this verse) actually look like for me at the moment of temptation, not just in retrospect?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before opening a social media app, pause and ask whether you are about to graze on someone's body for pleasure — and if so, close it and chant one round instead.
    • Refuse to join group chats, parties, or "banter" whose energy depends on rating or pursuing the opposite sex; bear the social cost consciously.
    • Find at least one senior devotee — not a peer — to whom you can speak honestly about attraction, dating pressure, and pornography, so lust does not grow in secret.
    householder
    • Examine whether you are relating to your spouse as a fellow soul on the way to Kṛṣṇa or merely as a reliable means of sense gratification, and adjust one concrete habit this week.
    • Set clear, mutually-agreed limits with colleagues, neighbors, and online contacts of the opposite sex — protect your marriage from the "stronger elephants" of flirtation, fantasy, and emotional affairs.
    • Teach your children early — through your own conduct more than lectures — that men and women are souls, not bodies to be displayed or consumed.
    senior
    • Use this stage to publicly and inwardly retire from any lingering exploitative attention to the opposite sex; let viveka, not biology, govern your eyes and speech.
    • Mentor younger men honestly about the wreckage you have seen lust cause, including in devotee circles, so they need not learn only by being trampled.
    • Loosen attachments to romantic memory and fantasy as carefully as attachments to property — both bind the soul as death approaches, and both can be offered at Kṛṣṇa's feet.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading the verse as anti-woman rather than anti-exploitation — the warning is about one's own lust and folly, not the woman's nature.
    • Treating the danger as merely external (jealous rivals, scandal) and missing that the deeper death is the death of viveka and bhakti.
    • Assuming celibates alone are addressed — householders too are warned against exploiting even their own spouse merely for sense gratification.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Modern culture insists that mutual consent makes any sensual arrangement safe — where does this verse cut against that assumption, and where might you honestly disagree?
    • Share a concrete situation where you sensed the "stronger elephants" closing in around a pursuit driven by lust — what did you do, and what do you wish you had done?
    • How should this verse be taught to a teenager today without it sounding either prudish or fear-based?
    Role Play

    A college-aged devotee tells his older godbrother that he has started messaging a woman he met online; he insists it is harmless friendship, but admits he checks her profile many times a day. The godbrother must respond using the logic of SB 11.8.14 — neither shaming him nor letting him drift — and help him see the bull-elephants already gathering.

    Objections & Responses
    • This verse is patriarchal — it blames women for men's lust and treats them as property to be fought over.
      The verse addresses the prājña man and diagnoses his folly, not the woman's worth; she is no more "to blame" than the she-elephant. Gauḍīya ācāryas read every soul as eternally part of Kṛṣṇa, and the warning here is precisely that exploitative lust degrades the exploiter and the exploited together.
    • In a modern consensual relationship there are no "rival elephants" — this danger is obsolete.
      The rivals are not only literal — they are jealousy, comparison, addiction, loss of discrimination, and the slow death of one's spiritual life. Crimes of passion, broken families, and ruined sādhana around us show that the elephant-herd is very much still there, only better dressed.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.15

    ↗
    न देयं नोपभोग्यं च लुब्धैर्यद् दु:खसञ्चितम् । भुङ्क्ते तदपि तच्चान्यो मधुहेवार्थविन्मधु ॥ १५ ॥
    na deyaṁ nopabhogyaṁ ca
    [na deyam nopabhogyam cha]
    lubdhair yad duḥkha-saṣcitam
    [lubdhair yad duhkha-sashchitam]
    bhuṅkte tad api tac cānyo
    [bhunkte tad api tach chaanyo]
    madhu-hevārthavin madhu
    [madhu-hevaarthavin madhu]
    Word-for-word
    na — not deyam — to be given in charity to others na — not upabhogyam — to be enjoyed personally ca — also lubdhaiḥ — by those who are greedy yat — that which duḥkha — with great struggle and pain saṣcitam — is accumulated bhuṅkte — he enjoys tat — that api — nevertheless tat — that ca — also anyaḥ — someone else madhu-hā — the one who steals the honey from the beehive iva — like artha — wealth vit — one who knows how to recognize madhu — the honey.

    madhu
    the honey.
    A greedy person accumulates a large quantity of money with great struggle and pain, but the person who has struggled so much to acquire this wealth is not always allowed to enjoy it himself or give it in charity to others. The greedy man is like the bee who struggles to produce a large quantity of honey, which is then stolen by a man who will enjoy it personally or sell it to others. No matter how carefully one hides his hard-earned wealth or tries to protect it, there are those who are expert in detecting the whereabouts of valuable things, and they will steal it.
    Purport

    One may argue that a wealthy man may conceal his money so expertly, by investing it in banks, stocks, properties, and so on, that there is no danger of theft. Only foolish people actually hide money by burying it in the ground or placing it under a mattress. But despite the fact that the majority of the world’s wealth has been accumulated in highly developed capitalistic countries, these countries are being severely challenged by many enemies who threaten at any moment to overrun the capitalists and steal their wealth. Similarly, we often find that the children of wealthy people are kidnapped, and then their parents must pay huge ransoms. Sometimes the parents themselves may also be kidnapped. Furthermore, there are so-called investment counselors who are expert in stealing the money of rich people; and in the modern age governments have also become expert in stealing money by taxation. Thus, the word artha-vit indicates one who by hook or by crook is expert in stealing the hard-earned wealth of other people. The bees work frantically to produce honey, but they will not enjoy their honey. As Lord Kṛṣṇa states, mṛtyuḥ sarva-haraś cāham: “I will come as death personified and steal everything.” ( Bg. 10.34 ) Somehow or other one’s hard-earned material opulence will be stolen, and therefore, as mentioned in this verse, one should not work uselessly like the foolish honeybee.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The wealth that greedy persons collect with great difficulty but neither enjoy nor spend is enjoyed by another person, just as the collector of honey, knowing how to attain his goal, takes honey collected by the bees. Wealth accumulated without giving or enjoying is taken by others. The bee keeper teaches this. Another person who is stronger enjoys what is collected by another person, just as a person enjoys honey gathered by the bees. “How can another person understand one has wealth if one hides it?” Some persons understand wealth by certain symptoms in another person and know how to get it (arthavit). The honey collector knows where to get the honey in the hollow of the tree by following the bees.
    Related values & verses

    BG 10.34
    Related lectures
    • MM SB 11-08-15 - 2017-05-05 ISKCON Alachua
      Her Grace Malati Mataji· 2017-05-05· English
    • Avatar Lila Pr SB 11-08-15 - Spiritual Greed Versus Material Greed - 2018-04-10 ISKCON Chowpatty
      His Grace Avatar Lila Prabhu· 2018-04-10· English
    Personal Guidance
    Hoarded wealth, like the bee's honey, is stolen by someone expert at taking it — Kṛṣṇa as death takes all, so do not labor uselessly.
    Practical Applications
    • Name one stash of wealth you are anxiously guarding, and today give a measurable portion of it in Kṛṣṇa's service before someone else takes it.
    • Refuse one act of greedy accumulation today — a purchase, an extra hour of overwork, a hoarding decision — and use the freed time or money for sādhana or charity.
    • Each evening, look at the day's earnings and ask whether you spent them, gave them, or merely guarded them like the honeybee, and adjust tomorrow accordingly.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where am I working frantically like the honeybee — producing wealth I will neither enjoy nor give away — and who is the artha-vit waiting to take it?
    • If Kṛṣṇa as mṛtyuḥ sarva-haraḥ will take everything, what am I still pretending I have securely stored against Him?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Notice the hours you pour into building a follower count, a GPA, or a resume line that you will not even remember in ten years, and redirect one of those hours today to hearing or chanting about Kṛṣṇa.
    • Refuse one purchase this week that is really about hoarding status — the extra sneaker, the upgraded phone, the trend buy — and quietly give that money toward Kṛṣṇa's service.
    • When a friend brags about an internship salary or a scholarship, practice not envying the honey; remind yourself someone expert will eventually take it, and ask what they are doing with their soul.
    householder
    • Sit with your spouse this week, look honestly at savings and investments, and decide together one fixed percentage that goes to Kṛṣṇa's service before any further accumulation.
    • Refuse one career move today that is driven purely by greedy accumulation — the extra job, the speculative bet, the overtime that steals time from children's spiritual training.
    • Teach your children by example: let them see you give in charity from real wealth, not from leftovers, so they learn early that hoarded honey is always taken.
    senior
    • Begin actively dismantling the hoard now — give meaningful wealth to Kṛṣṇa's service in your lifetime rather than leaving it for an artha-vit, a tax authority, or a quarreling heir to take.
    • Speak frankly with your family this month about your estate as an offering, not a legacy of accumulation, and name the portion that will go to devotional service.
    • Each morning, meditate on mṛtyuḥ sarva-haraś cāham from BG 10.34 — Kṛṣṇa as death takes all — and let that meditation loosen one more attachment to property, savings, or possessions before the day begins.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading this as a warning against the rich while quietly continuing the same hoarding pattern at one's own smaller scale.
    • Using "detachment from wealth" as an excuse for laziness or irresponsibility toward dependents the householder is duty-bound to maintain.
    • Becoming cynical about banks, governments, and taxes instead of redirecting the underlying energy toward giving in charity and serving Kṛṣṇa.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one specific way you are currently behaving like the honeybee in this verse — accumulating what you will not enjoy — and let the group press you on why.
    • Prabhupāda lists banks, stocks, kidnappers, investment counselors, and taxes as today's artha-vit; which of these do you secretly trust to protect you, and what would it mean to stop?
    • Where is the honest line for a householder between responsible saving for family and the greedy accumulation this verse condemns — and do you all agree on where it falls?
    Role Play

    One student plays a successful professional who has just received a large bonus and is deciding between investing it, upgrading the house, or giving a significant portion in Kṛṣṇa's service; another plays a Gauḍīya friend who has read SB 11.8.15 that morning. Run the conversation until a concrete decision is reached, then have the group critique whether the decision was honeybee logic or artha-vit-proof.

    Objections & Responses
    • This verse sounds like a recipe for poverty — if I do not accumulate, how will I support my family, educate my children, and care for aging parents?
      The verse does not forbid earning or saving; it condemns greedy accumulation that is neither enjoyed nor given. A Gauḍīya householder earns honestly, maintains dependents as a sacred duty, and channels the surplus into Kṛṣṇa's service — that is the opposite of the bee, who works frantically only to be robbed.
    • Modern financial instruments — diversified portfolios, insurance, trusts — really do protect wealth, so the bee analogy is outdated.
      Prabhupāda directly addresses this in the purport: banks, stocks, and properties only seem secure, but capitalistic nations are challenged by enemies, children are kidnapped, counselors steal, and governments tax — and finally Kṛṣṇa Himself says mṛtyuḥ sarva-haraś cāham (BG 10.34), I come as death and take everything. No instrument outlasts that artha-vit.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.16

    ↗
    सुदु:खोपार्जितैर्वित्तैराशासानां गृहाशिष: । मधुहेवाग्रतो भुङ्क्ते यतिर्वै गृहमेधिनाम् ॥ १६ ॥
    su-duḥkhopārjitair vittair
    [su-duhkhopaarjitair vittair]
    āśāsānāṁ gṛhāśiṣaḥ
    [aashaasaanaam grihaashishah]
    madhu-hevāgrato bhuṅkte
    [madhu-hevaagrato bhunkte]
    yatir vai gṛha-medhinām
    [yatir vai griha-medhinaam]
    Word-for-word
    su-duḥkha — with great struggle upārjitaiḥ — that which is acquired vittaiḥ — material opulence āśāsānām — of those fervently desiring gṛha — related to domestic enjoyment āśiṣaḥ — blessings madhu-hā — the person who steals honey from the bees iva — like agrataḥ — first, before others bhuṅkte — enjoys yatiḥ — a saintly mendicant vai — certainly gṛha-medhinām — of those dedicated to material family life.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    gṛha-medhinām
    of those dedicated to material family life.
    Just as a hunter takes away the honey laboriously produced by the honeybees, similarly, saintly mendicants such as brahmacārīs and sannyāsīs are entitled to enjoy the property painstakingly accumulated by householders dedicated to family enjoyment.
    Purport

    The scriptures state: “Saintly mendicants in the sannyāsa order of life and brahmacārīs have first right to enjoy the sumptuous foodstuffs produced by the householders. If the householders enjoy such foodstuffs without first offering them in charity to the mendicants, such neglectful householders must undergo the lunar fast called cāndrāyaṇam. ” In family life one must overcome the natural tendency toward selfishness by giving abundant charity. Modern society foolishly does not observe such Vedic injunctions, and as a result the world has become overrun by envious gṛha-medhīs, or persons wholly and solely dedicated to personal gratification in family life. Therefore, the whole world is seized in an uncontrollable spasm of violence and suffering. If one wants to live peacefully one must follow the Vedic injunctions regulating family life. Although the householders work very hard to accumulate money, the saintly sannyāsīs and brahmacārīs have the right to first enjoy the fruits of such labor. The conclusion is that one should give first priority to spiritual advancement in Kṛṣṇa consciousness and thus perfect one’s life. Then even without personal endeavor, one will be supplied all of one’s necessities by the mercy of the Personality of Godhead.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Just as a hunter takes away the honey laboriously produced by the honeybees, similarly, saintly mendicants such as brahmacārīs and sannyāsīs are entitled to enjoy the property painstakingly accumulated by householders dedicated to family enjoyment. One should enjoy without endeavor. The honey collector is the teacher for this. Āśāsānām should be āśāsānānām. yatiś ca brahmacārī ca pakvān na svāmināv ubhau | tayor annam adattvā tu bhuktvā cāndrāyaṇaṁ caret || The sannyāsī and brahmacārī should take the food cooked by the householder. If a housholder does not give them food, and eats it himself, he should perform atonement by observing the cāndrāyaṇa vow. Parāśara Smṛti 1.51
    Related lectures
    • Janmastami Pr SB 11-08-16 - 2017-05-08 ISKCON Alachua
      His Grace Janmastami Prabhu· 2017-05-08· English
    Personal Guidance
    Householders accumulate wealth through hard labor, yet the first fruits rightfully belong to sannyāsīs and brahmacārīs — give in charity before you enjoy.
    Practical Applications
    • Before your next meal at home, set aside a portion as an offering to Kṛṣṇa and His devotees, and only then sit down to eat.
    • Identify one sādhu, brahmacārī, or temple in your life this week and give substantial charity from your earned income — not the leftover, but the first cut.
    • Refuse the gṛha-medhī mentality by deciding today what fixed percentage of your income leaves the household for spiritual purposes before any personal spending.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my householder life am I behaving like a honeybee guarding the comb — hoarding what was never finally mine — and what is that grip costing my inner life?
    • If a saintly mendicant arrived at my door tonight, would my budget, my pantry, and my heart actually be arranged to honor his prior claim, or only my own enjoyment?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • From your first paycheck, internship stipend, or pocket money, fix a percentage that goes to Kṛṣṇa's service before it touches your spending — not after the phone, the trip, and the coffee.
    • When friends pool money for a birthday or trip, quietly arrange a parallel pool for the temple or a sādhu, and notice how differently the two purchases sit in your heart a week later.
    • Refuse the campus story that says "I earned it, it's mine" — practice offering even small wins, like a stipend or prize, before celebrating them on social media.
    householder
    • Sit with your spouse this week and rebuild the family budget so that charity to devotees and Kṛṣṇa's service is the first line item, not the last, and let lifestyle adjust around it.
    • When sannyāsīs, brahmacārīs, or temple devotees visit your home, feed them first and with the best preparations, and let your children see the order of priorities embodied.
    • Audit how much of your hard-earned wealth in the last year actually left the household for spiritual purposes versus accumulated as honey in the comb, and correct the ratio before the next quarter.
    senior
    • Restructure your estate and giving plan so that a significant portion of accumulated wealth supports bona fide devotees and Kṛṣṇa's mission, rather than only flowing to heirs.
    • Mentor a younger householder in this verse's principle — show them, from your own accounts, what proportional charity across a working life actually looks like.
    • Loosen your grip on the comb deliberately: identify one painstakingly built asset you are still guarding out of habit, and use it now, while you can see it employed in Kṛṣṇa's service.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Treating charity as the spare change left after lifestyle and savings, rather than as the first and rightful share of what was painstakingly earned.
    • Using this verse to justify lazy mendicancy or entitled demands on householders, instead of receiving their labor with gratitude and using it for genuine spiritual purposes.
    • Reducing the teaching to ritual food-offering while preserving an envious, gṛha-medhī heart that still treats family wealth as exclusively one's own.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share with your partner the last time you gave charity that genuinely pinched — and the last time you gave only what you would not miss; what does the gap reveal?
    • Prabhupāda calls the modern world an "uncontrollable spasm of violence and suffering" because gṛha-medhīs no longer honor this injunction — do you agree, and where do you see this in your own city?
    • Is it fair that those who do not earn the wealth have first right to enjoy it? Wrestle honestly with the instinct that says no.
    Role Play

    A working householder has just received an unexpected bonus. A brahmacārī from the local temple visits the same evening and mentions, without asking directly, that the temple kitchen is short on rice and ghee for the coming festival. One student plays the householder torn between a planned family vacation, mortgage prepayment, and the temple need; another plays the brahmacārī who must neither beg nor flatter; a third plays the householder's spouse weighing family security against this verse's injunction. Work toward a concrete decision, not a philosophical tie.

    Objections & Responses
    • Why should people who do not work get first claim on what I earned by my own sweat? This sounds like spiritualized freeloading.
      The sannyāsī and brahmacārī have renounced personal accumulation precisely so that the householder's wealth can be sanctified through their acceptance. They are not freeloaders but the channel by which your hard-earned vitta becomes connected to Kṛṣṇa; without that channel, the same money simply feeds the spasm of envious gṛha-medhī life that Prabhupāda warns produces violence and suffering.
    • In modern society there are frauds in saffron and genuine need among ordinary working people — why direct charity to mendicants rather than to the poor?
      Discrimination is required — give to those actually engaged in Kṛṣṇa's service, not to anyone in costume. But do not let the existence of frauds dissolve the principle; material relief addresses the body, while charity to bona fide sādhus addresses the soul of both giver and receiver, and a society that abandons the second will never solve the first.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.17

    ↗
    ग्राम्यगीतं न श‍ृणुयाद् यतिर्वनचर: क्व‍‍चित् । शिक्षेत हरिणाद् बद्धान्मृगयोर्गीतमोहितात् ॥ १७ ॥
    grāmya-gītaṁ na śṛṇuyād
    [graamya-geetam na shrinuyaad]
    yatir vana-caraḥ kvacit
    [yatir vana-charah kvachit]
    śikṣeta hariṇād baddhān
    [shiksheta harinaad baddhaan]
    mṛgayor gīta-mohitāt
    [mrigayor geeta-mohitaat]
    Word-for-word
    grāmya — relating to sense gratification gītam — songs na — not śṛṇuyāt — he should hear yatiḥ — a saintly mendicant vana — in the forest caraḥ — moving kvacit — ever śikṣeta — one should learn hariṇāt — from the deer baddhāt — bound up mṛgayoḥ — of the hunter gīta — by the song mohitāt — bewildered.
