    A saintly person dwelling in the forest in the renounced order of life should never listen to songs or music promoting material enjoyment. Rather, a saintly person should carefully study the example of the deer, who is bewildered by the sweet music of the hunter’s horn and is thus captured and killed.
    Purport

    If one becomes attached to the sense gratification of materialistic music and songs, one will develop all the features of material entanglement. One should hear Bhagavad-gītā, or the song sung by the Supreme Lord.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    A saintly person dwelling in the forest in the renounced order of life should never listen to songs or music promoting material enjoyment. Rather, he should carefully study the example of the deer, which is bewildered by the sweet music of the hunter’s horn and is thus captured and killed. He learned from the deer that attachment to material songs causes obstacles. One should instead hear Bhagavad-gītā.
    Related lectures
    • Balaram Shakti Pr SB 11-08-17-29 - GEV
      His Grace Balaram Shakti Prabhu· English
    • PSarasvati Sw SB 11-08-17-18 - 2017-05-10 ISKCON Alachua
      His Holiness Prabodhananda Sarasvati Swami· 2017-05-10· English
    • VedavyasaPSW SB 11-08-17 - 2009-05-13
      His Holiness Vedavyasa Priya Swami· 2009-05-13· English
    Personal Guidance
    Like the deer lured by the hunter's horn, a sādhaka caught by sense-pleasing music walks straight into bondage — hear Kṛṣṇa's song instead.
    Practical Applications
    • Audit today's playlist and delete the tracks that quietly inflame lust, envy, or self-pity.
    • Replace one stretch of grāmya-gīta listening with audible recitation or kīrtana of Bhagavad-gītā.
    • Notice the first pull toward sentimental music when restless, and turn instead to the holy name.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which songs do I defend as 'just entertainment' while knowing they pull me back into the consciousness Kṛṣṇa is calling me out of?
    • Where in my life am I the deer — standing transfixed by something sweet, unaware the arrow is already drawn?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before opening a streaming app, ask which mood you are about to feed — loneliness, lust, rebellion — and choose accordingly.
    • Pick one playlist tied to a past relationship or peer group and retire it; replace it with a kīrtana track you can actually enjoy.
    • When friends pass the aux cord and play something coarse, do not perform outrage — simply notice what it does to your mind for the next hour.
    householder
    • Notice what plays in the car with your children and ask whether those lyrics are forming the saṁskāras you want them to carry.
    • Replace background music at dinner one evening a week with a recorded reading of Bhagavad-gītā or Bhāgavatam.
    • When stress sends you toward sentimental or aggressive music as a release valve, treat that pull as a signal to chant a round instead.
    senior
    • Review the songs that still stir old longings or regrets, and decide which ones are no longer worth carrying into your remaining years.
    • Curate what your grandchildren hear in your home — your house can be the place they first encounter the song sung by the Lord.
    • As hearing itself softens with age, give your remaining attention to harināma and Bhāgavatam rather than to the music that shaped your earlier self.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Treating the verse as forbidding all music, then bingeing in secret rather than substituting Kṛṣṇa-saṅkīrtana.
    • Assuming the warning is only for forest-dwelling sannyāsīs and exempting one's own household listening.
    • Cleaning up the playlist outwardly while still replaying the same lyrics and moods internally.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one song or genre you suspect is your personal hunter's horn — and what you have actually tried to do about it.
    • Is there a difference between 'neutral' background music and grāmya-gīta, or is that distinction a self-deception we should examine?
    • When does art that depicts material life serve bhakti, and when does it simply rehearse viṣaya in the heart?
    Role Play

    Two devotees share a commute. One puts on a popular love song and says, 'It's just nostalgia, it doesn't affect my sādhana.' The other has been quietly disturbed by such music for weeks. Play out the conversation honestly — not as a lecture, but as two practitioners working out how this verse applies between them.

    Objections & Responses
    • This is puritanical. Music is a universal human good — surely Kṛṣṇa, who plays the flute, does not want us joyless.
      The verse does not forbid music; it forbids grāmya-gīta — music that glorifies sense gratification. Kṛṣṇa's flute and the saṅkīrtana of the holy name are the highest music. The point is not less song but the right song.
    • I can listen to anything and stay detached — my consciousness is strong enough.
      The deer also thought itself safe in its own forest. Prabhupāda's purport is direct: attachment to materialistic songs develops 'all the features of material entanglement.' The verse warns the renunciant precisely because confidence in one's own immunity is the first stage of capture.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.18

    ↗
    नृत्यवादित्रगीतानि जुषन् ग्राम्याणि योषिताम् । आसां क्रीडनको वश्य ऋष्यश‍ृङ्गो मृगीसुत: ॥ १८ ॥

    [rishyashringo mrigee-sutah]
    Word-for-word
    nṛtya — dancing vāditra — musical performance gītāni — songs juṣan — cultivating grāmyāni — related to sense gratification yoṣitām — of women āsām — of them krīḍanakaḥ — a plaything vaśyaḥ — fully controlled ṛṣya-śṛṅgaḥ — the sage Ṛṣyaśṛṅga mṛgī-sutaḥ — the son of a deer.

    mṛgī-sutaḥ
    the son of a deer.
    Becoming attracted to the worldly singing, dancing and musical entertainment of beautiful women, even the great sage Ṛṣyaśṛṅga, the son of a deer, fell totally under their control, just like a pet animal.
    Purport

    Ṛṣyaśṛṅga was intentionally brought up by his father in an atmosphere of complete innocence. The father thought that if his son were never exposed to the sight of women he would always remain a perfect brahmacārī. But by chance the inhabitants of the neighboring kingdom, who were suffering from a long-term drought, received divine advice that rain would return to their kingdom only after the brāhmaṇa named Ṛṣyaśṛṅga stepped foot in it. Therefore they sent beautiful women to Ṛṣyaśṛṅga’s hermitage to entice him and bring him back with them. Since Ṛṣyaśṛṅga had never even heard about women, he easily fell for their trap.

    The name Ṛṣyaśṛṅga indicates that the young sage was born with a deerlike horn growing out of his forehead. If like the deer a ṛṣi becomes attracted to sweet musical sounds promising sense gratification, then like the deer he is quickly vanquished. A thoughtful person should humbly take instruction from the deer, who is doomed by attraction to musical sense gratification.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Becoming attracted to the worldly singing, dancing and musical entertainment of beautiful women, even the great sage Ṛṣyaśṛṅga, the son of Mṛgī, fell totally under their control, just like a pet animal. An example of attachment to material songs is given.
    Related lectures
    • VedavyasaPSW SB 11-08-18 - Get tuned to transcendental sound only - 2009-05-14
      His Holiness Vedavyasa Priya Swami· 2009-05-14· English
    Personal Guidance
    Even a lifelong brahmacārī like Ṛṣyaśṛṅga was tamed like a pet by sweet sounds and beautiful company — guard against the entry-points of sense gratification.
    Practical Applications
    • Identify one form of music, video, or entertainment that softens your resolve, and refuse it for the next seven days.
    • Notice the specific sense-channel — eyes, ears, taste — through which māyā most easily leads you, and place a concrete restraint there today.
    • When sweet stimulation arises and pulls you, pause and remember Ṛṣyaśṛṅga the deer-son, then return to chanting or service.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which 'innocent' pleasure in my day is quietly domesticating me into māyā's pet, even as I imagine myself a sādhaka?
    • Where am I trusting my untested renunciation, assuming that what I have never faced cannot conquer me?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Audit your phone for the one app, playlist, or feed that most reliably erodes your sādhana, and delete or block it before the end of today.
    • When friends pull you toward a party, concert, or hangout that you know dulls your resolve, practice saying no once this week without explaining or apologizing.
    • Replace one hour of background entertainment — music while studying, videos while eating — with chanting or reading, and notice what changes in your focus.
    householder
    • Look honestly at the entertainment your household consumes together — shows, music, social media — and remove one item that is quietly shaping your children's taste toward viṣaya.
    • When a work or social event involves grāmya music and revelry, decide in advance how long you will stay and what you will not partake of, rather than trusting your willpower on the spot.
    • Build one daily family ritual — ārati, kīrtana, reading aloud — that retrains the family's ears toward Kṛṣṇa-centered sweetness instead of marketplace sweetness.
    senior
    • Notice the 'harmless' indulgences — favorite songs, nostalgic films, rich food, long conversations — that you have justified in retirement, and ask which of them are tightening rather than loosening attachment.
    • When mentoring younger devotees, share honestly the specific sense-pulls that domesticated you over a lifetime, so they do not repeat the pattern of Ṛṣyaśṛṅga's untested innocence.
    • Spend the freed hours of this stage in hari-kīrtana, śravaṇa, and the company of devotees, deliberately starving the senses of grāmya input as you prepare for the body's final transition.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Assuming that because a sense object seems harmless or cultured, it cannot bind the heart — the verse shows even a sage was caught.
    • Mistaking sheltered inexperience for actual vairāgya, and walking unguarded into situations one has never trained the senses against.
    • Treating the warning as being only about gross lust, missing that subtler refined entertainments — music, art, ambience — work the same trap.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one form of 'sweet sound' in your life — a playlist, a show, a conversation circle — that you suspect is taming you, and discuss whether you would actually give it up.
    • Ṛṣyaśṛṅga's father tried to protect him by complete shelter, and it failed — debate whether modern parents and teachers should shelter the young or deliberately train them under exposure.
    • When does cultivated aesthetic enjoyment — classical music, fine food, beautiful surroundings — cross from sattva into viṣaya, and how do you tell in your own life?
    Role Play

    One student plays a sincere brahmacārī who insists he is 'past' the pull of entertainment because he never watches anything; another student plays a friend who hands him earbuds with a beautiful new song, saying it is just art. Play out the next two minutes of conversation, then swap roles and try again with the brahmacārī responding from the lesson of Ṛṣyaśṛṅga.

    Objections & Responses
    • This is an outdated, anti-woman verse — it blames women for a man's lack of self-control.
      The verse is not blaming women; it is warning the practitioner about his own untrained senses. Śukadeva uses Ṛṣyaśṛṅga because he was the most innocent of men, so the lesson lands on the sādhaka — even a sinless sage was undone by his own attraction to sweet sounds. The mirror is held up to us, not to anyone else.
    • Music, dance, and beauty are gifts of Kṛṣṇa's energy — why treat them as enemies?
      Music and beauty in Kṛṣṇa's service — kīrtana, deity worship, līlā-kathā — purify the heart. The verse warns specifically about grāmya, worldly entertainment aimed at sense gratification, which trains the senses to chase viṣaya. The same ear that thrills to a sensual song can be retrained to thrill to the holy name; the question is which one we are feeding.

    just as.
    Just as a fish, incited by the desire to enjoy his tongue, is fatally trapped on the fisherman’s hook, similarly, a foolish person is bewildered by the extremely disturbing urges of the tongue and thus is ruined.
    Purport

    The fisherman places meaty bait on a sharp hook and easily attracts the unintelligent fish, who is greedy to enjoy its tongue. Similarly, people are mad after gratifying their tongues and lose all discrimination in their eating habits. For momentary gratification they construct huge slaughterhouses and kill millions of innocent creatures, and by inflicting such atrocious suffering they prepare a ghastly future for themselves. But even if one eats only the foods authorized in the Vedas, there is still danger. One may eat too sumptuously and then the artificially stuffed belly will create pressure on the sexual organs. Thus one will fall down into the lower modes of nature and commit sinful activities that lead to the death of one’s spiritual life. From the fish one should carefully learn the real dangers involved in gratifying the tongue.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Just as a fish, incited by the desire to enjoy his tongue, is fatally trapped on the fisherman’s hook, similarly, a foolish person is bewildered by the extremely disturbing urges of the tongue and thus meets his death. Attraction to material taste is a cause of obstacles. This he learned from the fish. Man, bewildered by his tongue which is agitating and difficult to control (atipramāthinyā) attains death. The fish attains death by the hook with tasty bait.
    Related lectures
    • VedavyasaPSW SB 11-08-19-21 - Krsna-prasada sevaya ki jai - 2009-05-15
      His Holiness Vedavyasa Priya Swami· 2009-05-15· English
    Personal Guidance
    Like a fish hooked by bait, the unintelligent are dragged toward ruin by the agitated tongue — discipline the jihvā or it will eat your spiritual life.
    Practical Applications
    • Before your next meal, offer the food to Kṛṣṇa as prasāda and eat only what serves sādhana, not what excites the tongue.
    • Refuse to participate in flesh-eating today, recognizing that tongue-pleasure paid for by another's suffering prepares a ghastly future for yourself.
    • Notice when you reach for a second helping out of rasa-greed rather than need, and stop — overeating swells the belly and drags the senses downward.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my eating and speaking has the tongue quietly become the fisherman's hook, pulling me away from Kṛṣṇa one bite or one comment at a time?
    • If I traced my last serious fall in sādhana backward, would I find an unguarded tongue at the start of that chain?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before opening a food-delivery app or a snack drawer late at night, pause and ask whether you are actually hungry or just chasing a rasa — and choose water and japa instead even once this week.
    • Refuse to let peer pressure at hostels, hangouts, or canteens drag you into non-offerable food; carry prasāda or simple home food and be unembarrassed about it.
    • Watch how short-form food content and mukbang-style videos train your tongue to crave — unfollow a few of those accounts today and replace them with kīrtana or class audio.
    householder
    • Set a family rule that meat, fish, and eggs do not enter the kitchen, and explain to children plainly why — connecting tongue-discipline to the slaughterhouse economy this verse foresaw.
    • Cook and serve smaller, sattvic, offered meals at home rather than frequent restaurant outings, so the household budget and the family's senses are both protected from rasa-greed.
    • When entertaining guests or closing business deals over food, take a modest plate and stop on time — do not let hospitality or networking become the bait that overfeeds and dulls your evening sādhana.
    senior
    • Use this stage of life to deliberately simplify the palate — fewer dishes, less salt, less sugar, more prasāda — recognizing that an old body chasing strong flavors is the fish still nibbling the hook.
    • Speak less, and when you speak to children and grandchildren, let the tongue serve hari-kathā and honest counsel rather than complaint, criticism, or nostalgia for past enjoyments.
    • Mentor a younger devotee or family member specifically on tongue-control — eating, drinking, and speaking — passing on what you have learned about how this one sense quietly ruined or saved your spiritual life.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Treating this verse as merely a dietary rule about vegetarianism while ignoring the deeper warning about rasa-vimohitaḥ — the bewilderment of seeking flavors as the goal.
    • Assuming that because the food is offered and Vedic-authorized, quantity no longer matters — Prabhupāda warns that even sumptuous prasāda overstuffs the belly and inflames the lower senses.
    • Reducing the tongue's danger to taste alone and forgetting its other function — speech — so that gossip, joking, and careless words go uncorrected.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one food or flavor you know agitates your sādhana, and discuss honestly why you have not yet given it up.
    • Where does our community draw the line between honoring prasāda and indulging the tongue under a spiritual label — and do we agree on where that line sits?
    • If the fish cannot see the hook inside the bait, what are the hooks hidden inside the foods, restaurants, and food-content we consume daily?
    Role Play

    Two devotees are at a wedding reception. The table is full of opulent preparations, some offered, some not, and the host is personally urging a second and third plate. One devotee wants to eat freely, citing that it is a Vaiṣṇava festival; the other wants to take a small plate and stop. Play out the conversation between them — and then with the insistent host — using this verse to guide the response without becoming self-righteous.

    Objections & Responses
    • This is just ancient asceticism — modern nutrition science says enjoying food is healthy, and guilt around eating is harmful.
      The verse is not against eating or even against pleasure; it is against being rasa-vimohitaḥ, bewildered by flavor to the point of losing discrimination. A Gauḍīya does not eat with guilt — he eats Kṛṣṇa-prasāda with gratitude and a limit. The fish is not condemned for being hungry; it is hooked because it could not distinguish bait from food. Modern overeating, slaughterhouse industries, and food addictions are exactly the hook Prabhupāda is pointing to.
    • Singling out the tongue feels arbitrary — why not the eyes or the mind, which seem more powerful?
      Śāstra and Prabhupāda repeatedly identify the tongue as the most difficult sense to control and the gateway sense — once it is satisfied with prasāda and kṛṣṇa-kathā, the other senses fall in line; once it is indulged, the belly swells, the genitals are inflamed, and the whole sensory apparatus follows downward. That is why this verse uses the fish, not a deer or a moth — the tongue's hook is the one almost no one sees coming.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.20

    इन्द्रियाणि जयन्त्याशु निराहारा मनीषिण: । वर्जयित्वा तु रसनं तन्निरन्नस्य वर्धते ॥ २० ॥

    vardhate
    increases.
    By fasting, learned men quickly bring all of the senses except the tongue under control, because by abstaining from eating such men are afflicted with an increased desire to gratify the sense of taste.
    Purport

    In South America there is a saying that when the belly is full the heart is content. Thus, one who is eating sumptuously is jolly, and if one is deprived of proper food one’s appetite becomes even more voracious. An intelligent person, however, does not fall under the control of the tongue, but rather tries to make progress in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. By accepting the remnants of food offered to the Lord ( prasādam ), one gradually purifies the heart and automatically becomes simple and austere.

    In this connection, Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura states that the business of the tongue is to gratify itself with the varieties of flavor, but by wandering in the twelve holy forests of Vraja-maṇḍala (Vṛndāvana), one can be freed from the twelve flavors of material sense gratification. The five principal divisions of material relationships are neutral admiration, servitude, friendship, parental affection and conjugal love; the seven subordinate features of material relationships are material humor, astonishment, chivalry, compassion, anger, dread and ghastliness. Originally, these twelve rasas, or flavors of relationships, are exchanged between the Supreme Personality of Godhead and the living entity in the spiritual world; and by wandering in the twelve forests of Vṛndāvana one can respiritualize the twelve flavors of personal existence. Thus one will become a liberated soul, free from all material desires. If one artificially tries to give up sense gratification, especially that of the tongue, the attempt will be a failure, and in fact one’s desire for sense gratification will increase as a result of artificial deprivation. Only by experiencing real, spiritual pleasure in relationship with Kṛṣṇa can one give up material desires.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    By fasting, learned men quickly bring all of the senses except the tongue under control, because by abstaining from eating such men are afflicted with an increased desire to gratify the sense of taste. The moth is destroyed by attraction to form. The bee is destroyed by attraction to smell. The elephant is destroyed by attraction to touch. The deer is destroyed by attraction to sound. The fish is destroyed by attraction to taste. It has been said: kuraṅga-mātaṅga-pataṅga-bhṛṅga- mīnā hatāḥ paṣcabhir eva paṣca | ekaḥ pramādī sa kathaṁ na hanyate yaḥ sevate paṣcabhir eva paṣca || The deer, elephant, moth, bee and fish are destroyed by the five sense objects. How can an insane person who engages all senses in the five sense objects not be destroyed? Garuḍa Purāṇa 1.115.21 The tongue which endows the other senses with their functions (by eating food) is the cause of obstacles. Therefore one should make efforts to control it. This is explained in two verses. If one gives up eating, one conquers all the senses except the tongue. The urge of the tongue increases by fasting. If one eats, all senses become agitated by the attachment to taste. Therefore, one must conquer the tongue. Then all the senses following it will be conquered. One can control the tongue by developing a taste for chanting loudly the name of the Lord. It is said viṣayā vinivartante nirāhārasya dehinaḥ | rasa-varjaṁ raso ’py asya paraṁ dṛṣṭvā nivartate || One who refrains from eating also stops the sense objects from functioning, but the desire for tasting remains. Having experienced the highest taste, one can stop the desire for material taste. BG 2.59 :

      Her Grace Narayani Mataji· 2017-05-11· English

      His Holiness Jayadvaita Swami· English
    • RGS SB 11-08-20-33 Hindi - Twenty-Four Guru-21
      His Holiness Radha Govinda Swami· English
    Personal Guidance
    Fasting subdues every sense except the tongue, whose hunger only sharpens; the tongue is conquered by tasting Kṛṣṇa's prasādam, not by deprivation.
    Practical Applications
    • Before your next meal, offer the food to Kṛṣṇa and eat only the remnants as prasādam, treating taste as a gift to be received, not chased.
    • Notice the moment hunger turns into craving for a specific flavor, and instead of denying or indulging it, redirect that attention to chanting Kṛṣṇa's names.
    • Refuse the macho fast that quietly fantasizes about the meal afterward; eat simply, on time, and let the tongue serve kīrtana rather than rule the day.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my sādhana have I been controlling outward behavior while the tongue, in food or in speech, still runs the house unchecked?
    • What spiritual taste in relation to Kṛṣṇa would have to actually arise in me before material flavors lost their grip?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • When friends pressure you into another late-night food run or trendy diet, quietly cook or order something simple, offer it to Kṛṣṇa, and eat it as prasādam instead of as performance.
    • Before scrolling food, mukbang, or restaurant reels, ask whether you are actually hungry or just feeding the tongue through the eyes, and switch to even five minutes of chanting Kṛṣṇa's names.
    • Watch your speech-tongue in group chats and classrooms; refuse one piece of gossip or sarcastic put-down today and notice how that restraint steadies your study and mood.
    householder
    • Make the family kitchen a place of offering: cook with the intention that Kṛṣṇa eats first, and serve children and spouse prasādam, so taste is trained at home rather than outsourced to restaurants.
    • When work stress drives you toward comfort eating, alcohol, or compulsive snacking late at night, pause, drink water, chant a round, and only then decide if you actually need to eat.
    • Audit your speech-tongue with your spouse and children this week; cut one habitual complaint or harsh tone, and replace it with one specific appreciation rooted in seeing them as souls.
    senior
    • As the body's appetite naturally narrows, do not chase richer flavors to compensate; simplify to small, regular portions of Kṛṣṇa-prasādam and let that simplicity become part of your preparation for death.
    • Use mealtimes as deliberate sādhana: offer the plate, remember that you are eating the Lord's mercy, and let younger family members see an elder who is governed by remembrance rather than by taste.
    • Guard the tongue's other function in your role as elder; refuse to use age as license for blunt criticism or nostalgic complaint, and instead spend that breath on hari-kathā and blessings for the next generation.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Treating fasting as the goal itself and feeling proud of the deprivation, while the mind grows more obsessed with food than before.
    • Swinging between strict fasts and compensatory feasts, mistaking the oscillation for austerity instead of unchecked rasanā.
    • Forgetting that the tongue has two functions, taste and speech, and disciplining the eating while letting gossip, complaint, and flattery run free.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share an honest example of an austerity you took up that secretly increased the very desire it was meant to curb, and what you think went wrong.
    • Prabhupāda says the tongue is conquered through prasādam, not deprivation; where does that clash with the willpower-and-discipline model you grew up with?
    • If material relationships carry twelve rasas that are distortions of relationships with Kṛṣṇa, which rasa most often hijacks your tongue, and how would you begin to respiritualize it?
    Role Play

    One student plays a sincere new devotee who has been fasting two days a week and is now irritable, secretly browsing food videos, and proud of the fast; the other plays a senior devotee who must, using this verse, redirect them from suppression toward a prasādam-centered, Kṛṣṇa-conscious discipline without shaming the effort.

    Objections & Responses
    • Modern nutrition and psychology show that restrictive eating backfires; isn't this verse just confirming that fasting is a bad idea?
      The verse agrees that mere deprivation backfires, which is exactly why Gauḍīya practice does not stop at fasting; it replaces empty restraint with honoring Kṛṣṇa's prasādam, so the tongue is satisfied by a higher taste rather than starved into rebellion.
    • Why single out the tongue? Lust or anger seem far more destructive than enjoying good food.
      Prabhupāda and Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura point out that the tongue is the gateway sense: once it is pacified through prasādam and the chanting of Kṛṣṇa's names, the other senses, including those tied to lust and anger, lose their fuel and come under control almost on their own.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.21

    ↗
    तावज्जितेन्द्रियो न स्याद् विजितान्येन्द्रिय: पुमान् । न जयेद् रसनं यावज्जितं सर्वं जिते रसे ॥ २१ ॥
    tāvaj jitendriyo na syād
    [taavaj jitendriyo na syaad]
    vijitānyendriyaḥ pumān
    [vijitaanyendriyah pumaan]
    na jayed rasanaṁ yāvaj
    [na jayed rasanam yaavaj]
    jitaṁ sarvaṁ jite rase
    [jitam sarvam jite rase]
    Word-for-word
    tāvat — still jita-indriyaḥ — one who has conquered the senses na — not syāt — can be vijita-anya-indriyaḥ — one who has conquered all of the other senses pumān — a human being na jayet — cannot conquer rasanam — the tongue yāvat — as long as jitam — conquered sarvam — everything jite — when conquered rase — the tongue.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    the tongue.
    Although one may conquer all of the other senses, as long as the tongue is not conquered it cannot be said that one has controlled his senses. However, if one is able to control the tongue, then one is understood to be in full control of all the senses.
    Purport

    By eating, one gives energy and activity to all of the senses, and thus if the tongue is uncontrolled all of the senses will be dragged down to the material platform of existence. Therefore, by all means one must control the tongue. If one fasts, then all of the other senses become weak and lose their potency. The tongue, however, becomes more greedy to taste delicious preparations, and when one finally indulges the tongue, all of the senses quickly go out of control. Therefore, Śrīla Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura advises that one accept mahā-prasādam, or the remnants of the Lord’s food, in a moderate proportion. Since the tongue’s function is also to vibrate, one should vibrate the glorious holy name of the Supreme Lord and taste the ecstasy of pure Kṛṣṇa consciousness. As stated in Bhagavad-gītā, rasa-varjaṁ raso ’py asya paraṁ dṛṣṭvā nivartate: only by the higher taste of Kṛṣṇa consciousness can one give up the deadly lower taste that keeps one imprisoned in material bondage.

    Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura states that as long as one’s intelligence is materially covered, one cannot understand the pleasure of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Trying to enjoy without Kṛṣṇa, the living entity leaves the abode of the Supreme Lord, called Vrajabhūmi, and comes down into the material world, where he quickly loses control of his senses. One becomes particularly victimized by the tongue, belly and genitals, which exert intolerable pressure on the conditioned soul. These desires subside, however, when one reestablishes one’s blissful relationship with the Lord, who is actually the reservoir of all pleasure. One who is attached to the taste of Kṛṣṇa consciousness automatically follows all of the rules and regulations of religious life due to spontaneous attraction to viśuddha-sattva, or the mode of pure goodness. Without such spontaneous attraction, one is certainly bewildered by the pushing of the material senses.

    Even the beginning stage of devotional service, called sādhana-bhakti (regulatory practice), is so powerful that it brings one to the platform of anartha-nivṛtti, where one becomes free from unwanted sinful habits and gains relief from the pressure of the tongue, belly and genitals. Thus one is delivered from the bondage of material addiction and can no longer be cheated by the allurements of the material energy. As it is said, all that glitters is not gold. In this regard Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura recommends that we consider the following song written by his father, Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura:

    śarīra avidyā-jāl, jaḍendriya tāhe kāl, jīve phele viṣaya-sāgare tā’ra madhye jihvā ati, lobhamay sudurmati, tā’ke jetā kaṭhina saṁsāre

    kṛṣṇa baḍa dayāmay, karibāre jihvā jay, sva-prasādānna dila bhāi sei annāmṛta pāo, rādhā-kṛṣṇa-guṇa gāo, preme ḍāko caitanya-nitāi

    “O Lord, this material body is a lump of ignorance, and the senses are a network of paths to death. Somehow, we have fallen into this ocean of material sense enjoyment, and of all the senses the tongue is most voracious and uncontrollable; it is very difficult to conquer the tongue in this world. But You, dear Kṛṣṇa, are very kind to us and have given us such nice prasādam, just to control the tongue. Now we take this prasādam to our full satisfaction and glorify Their Lordships Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa, and in love call for the help of Lord Caitanya and Lord Nityānanda.”

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Although one may conquer all of the other senses, as long as the tongue is not conquered it cannot be said that one has controlled his senses. However, if one is able to control the tongue, then one is understood to be in full control of all the senses.
    Related lectures
    • GKG Hindi SB 11-08-21 - 2007-04-22 New Delhi
      His Holiness Gopal Krishna Goswami· 2007-04-22· Hindi
    • PSarasvati Sw SB 11-08-21 - 2017-05-12 ISKCON Alachua
      His Holiness Prabodhananda Sarasvati Swami· 2017-05-12· English
    Personal Guidance
    Until the tongue is conquered, no sense is truly conquered; control the tongue through prasādam and the holy name, and all senses follow.
    Practical Applications
    • Eat only kṛṣṇa-prasādam today, in moderate quantity, and refuse any food taken purely for tongue-pleasure.
    • When the tongue itches for stray talk, redirect it: chant the holy name aloud or speak something about Kṛṣṇa instead.
    • Notice the first moment of craving for a "special taste" today, and offer that exact item to Kṛṣṇa before tasting it.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which single tongue-indulgence — in food or in speech — am I quietly unwilling to give up, and what is it costing my sādhana?
    • Where am I mistaking strict fasting or austerity for sense control, while the tongue remains untouched and greedy underneath?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before opening a food-delivery app or scrolling restaurant reels, pause and ask whether this is hunger or just the tongue hunting — and choose prasādam from home instead.
    • Pick one kind of speech your tongue loves — gossip about classmates, sarcastic group-chat comments, or hot takes online — and fast from it for one full day.
    • When friends pressure you to "just try" something non-offered at a hostel mess, party, or café, practice a clean, non-preachy refusal today, so the tongue learns who is in charge.
    householder
    • Cook at least one meal today as an offering to Kṛṣṇa — plan it, prepare it, offer it, and only then serve the family — so the kitchen itself becomes a place of tongue-control.
    • Watch your tongue at the dinner table: refuse the habit of complaining about food, criticizing absent relatives, or rehearsing office grievances, and instead introduce one topic of Kṛṣṇa-kathā.
    • Sit with your spouse and identify one shared indulgence — a weekly restaurant ritual, a snack habit, a recurring argumentative topic — and replace it for one month with a shared prasādam meal and kīrtana.
    senior
    • Simplify your plate: today, eat less, eat only prasādam, and notice how loosening the tongue's grip also loosens the body's grip on you.
    • Use the tongue's remaining strength for its higher function — chant your rounds attentively and speak Kṛṣṇa-kathā to a grandchild, neighbor, or younger devotee.
    • Review the foods, drinks, and conversational habits you have always told yourself you would give up "later" — and give up one of them now, as preparation for the final passage.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Treating the verse as a diet rule and reducing it to vegetarianism, while ignoring the tongue's other function — careless, critical, or sense-gratifying speech.
    • Suppressing the tongue by force through fasting, which only intensifies its hunger, instead of feeding it the higher taste of prasādam and the holy name.
    • Assuming that because one chants and eats prasādam mechanically, the tongue is already conquered, when in fact it is still hunting for delicious preparations and idle talk.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one food or one kind of talk where your tongue still rules you — and notice whether the group's honesty matches your own.
    • Is it really true that conquering the tongue conquers all the other senses? Argue the case from your own experience, for and against.
    • Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura calls the tongue lobhamay sudurmati — greedy and very wicked. Where in modern "foodie" and "free speech" culture do you see this most clearly, and where are you complicit?
    Role Play

    A devotee is invited to a colleague's birthday dinner at a famous restaurant; everyone is ordering elaborate non-offered dishes and pressing him to "just enjoy this once." One student plays the devotee, another the insistent colleague, a third a curious friend who asks why prasādam matters. Run the scene twice — first letting the tongue win, then letting the higher taste win — and debrief what changed inside.

    Objections & Responses
    • This is just religious dieting dressed up as philosophy — what does what I eat have to do with spiritual life?
      The verse is not about diet but about which sense governs you. The tongue drives both eating and speaking, and through them energizes every other sense; if that one is loose, the rest follow it down. Prasādam and the holy name are not a diet but a transfer of the tongue's allegiance from viṣaya to Kṛṣṇa, and from that one shift the whole sensory life reorganizes.
    • Suppressing desires is unhealthy — modern psychology says we should accept and integrate them, not conquer them.
      Gauḍīya practice agrees that raw suppression fails; that is why Prabhupāda quotes rasa-varjaṁ raso 'py asya paraṁ dṛṣṭvā nivartate — the lower taste leaves only when a higher taste is actually tasted. We do not white-knuckle the tongue; we feed it mahā-prasādam and the holy name until viśuddha-sattva makes the old cravings genuinely uninteresting. That is integration at the level of the soul, not mere repression.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.22

    ↗
    पिङ्गला नाम वेश्यासीद् विदेहनगरे पुरा । तस्या मे शिक्षितं किञ्चिन्निबोध नृपनन्दन ॥ २२ ॥
    piṅgalā nāma veśyāsīd
    [pingalaa naama veshyaaseed]
    videha-nagare purā
    [videha-nagare puraa]
    tasyā me śikṣitaṁ kiṣcin
    [tasyaa me shikshitam kishchin]
    nibodha nṛpa-nandana
    [nibodha nripa-nandana]
    Word-for-word
    piṅgalā nāma — of the name Piṅgalā veśyā — a prostitute āsīt — there was videha-nagare — in the city named Videha purā — in the past tasyāḥ — from her me — by me śikṣitaṁ — what was learned kiṣcit — something nibodha — now you learn nṛpa-nandana — O son of kings.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    O son of kings.
    O son of kings, previously in the city of Videha there dwelled a prostitute named Piṅgalā. Now please hear what I have learned from that lady.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    O son of kings! Previously in the city of Videha there dwelled a prostitute named Piṅgalā. Now please hear what I have learned from that lady. From Piṅgalā he learned distaste for material enjoyment. Her story is now told.
    Related lectures
    • BhaktiVasudeva Sw SB 11-08-22-27 - Why to Cultivate Detachment in Our Life - 2018-04-18 ISKCON Chowpatty
      His Holiness Bhakti Vasudeva Swami· 2018-04-18· English
    • JAS SB 11-08-22
      His Holiness Jayadvaita Swami· English
    • VedavyasaPSW SB 11-08-22-27 - 2009-05-16
      His Holiness Vedavyasa Priya Swami· 2009-05-16· English
    • 2019-01-19 SB 11-08-22 Hindi - The Story of Prostitute - Savyasachi Pr ISKCON Bhopal
      ISKCON Bhopal· 2019-01-19· English
    Personal Guidance
    Even a prostitute in Videha became a teacher — Piṅgalā shows that disgust with material hope, when surrendered, ripens into bhakti.
    Practical Applications
    • Name today the one ‘client’ — person, position, paycheck — from whom you are still hoping for the happiness only Kṛṣṇa can give.
    • When the next wave of disappointment hits, refuse to numb it; treat it as Kṛṣṇa loosening a grip you could not loosen yourself.
    • Sit before your altar tonight and audibly transfer the longing you usually aim at the world toward Kṛṣṇa, in plain words.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which ‘Videha street’ do I still pace nightly — what specific hope keeps me waiting at the door of a passerby?
    • What would it cost me — in identity, income, or image — to admit that the source I have been soliciting cannot deliver, and to turn fully to Kṛṣṇa?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Identify the one social media account or group chat where you keep checking for validation, and fast from it for the next twenty-four hours.
    • When a crush, friend group, or grade fails to deliver the happiness you expected, write down what you were really hoping for and offer that longing to Kṛṣṇa before bed.
    • Speak honestly with one trusted friend about a ‘Piṅgalā street’ in your life — a pattern of seeking approval — instead of pretending everything is fine.
    householder
    • Notice this week the specific family member, client, or boss from whom you are still expecting the recognition only Kṛṣṇa can give, and adjust your inner expectation accordingly.
    • When a financial or career hope collapses, refuse to immediately rebuild the same hope elsewhere; sit with the emptiness in front of the Deity for at least one evening.
    • Tell your spouse or a close devotee friend, in plain language, one area where you have been ‘soliciting clients’ and ask them to help you turn that energy toward sādhana.
    senior
    • Make a written list of the worldly hopes you are quietly still holding — a child’s success, a legacy, a final recognition — and, one by one, formally hand them to Kṛṣṇa.
    • When the body or health disappoints today, treat it as Piṅgalā’s sleepless night and use the wakeful hours for japa instead of brooding.
    • Mentor one younger person not by giving them your worldly formulas, but by sharing the specific disappointments that finally turned your face toward Kṛṣṇa.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Romanticizing Piṅgalā’s renunciation while quietly keeping our own ‘arrangements’ intact and unexamined.
    • Treating disgust with the world as the goal itself, instead of as the doorway to active surrender to Kṛṣṇa.
    • Looking down on fallen people we meet, forgetting that the avadhūta openly accepted a veśyā as his guru.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one ‘Piṅgalā moment’ in your life — a disillusionment that, in hindsight, was Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, and one that you are still resisting.
    • If the avadhūta could learn from a prostitute, what does that say about whom we currently refuse to learn from, and why?
    • Where in your sādhana are you still ‘soliciting clients’ — performing devotion in hope of a material return — and how would the group recognize it in each other?
    Role Play

    Two students: one plays a friend who has just been passed over for a promotion they staked their identity on and is bitter; the other plays a fellow practitioner trying, without preaching, to help them see this disappointment as a Piṅgalā-style invitation rather than a punishment. Swap roles after five minutes.

    Objections & Responses
    • Why elevate a prostitute as a teacher? This sounds like a romantic story, not real guidance for serious life.
      The verse is not romanticizing her trade; it is honoring the moment she saw through it. Kṛṣṇa, through the avadhūta, is telling Yadu — and us — that the qualification of a guru is clear seeing, not social position. To dismiss her is to keep our own pride intact and miss the lesson.
    • I don’t feel any disgust with the world — I am reasonably content. Does this verse even apply to me?
      Contentment built on a steady supply of small material returns is exactly Piṅgalā’s situation before her turning. The verse is a gentle warning: do not wait for total collapse to look toward Kṛṣṇa. Begin transferring the hope now, while you still have the strength to choose.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.23

    ↗
    सा स्वैरिण्येकदा कान्तं सङ्केत उपनेष्यती । अभूत् काले बहिर्द्वारे बिभ्रती रूपमुत्तमम् ॥ २३ ॥
    sā svairiṇy ekadā kāntaṁ
    [saa svairiny ekadaa kaantam]
    saṅketa upaneṣyatī
    [sanketa upaneshyatee]
    abhūt kāle bahir dvāre
    [abhoot kaale bahir dvaare]
    bibhratī rūpam uttamam
    [bibhratee roopam uttamam]
    Word-for-word
    sā — she svairiṇī — the prostitute ekadā — once kāntam — a paying lover or a customer saṅkete — into her house of sex upaneṣyatī — to bring abhūt — she stood kāle — at night bahiḥ — outside dvāre — in the doorway bibhratī — holding up rūpam — her form uttamam — most beautiful.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    sā svairiṇy ekadā kāntaṁ
    sā
    she
    svairiṇī
    the prostitute
    ekadā
    once
    kāntam

    saṅketa upaneṣyatī

    into her house of sex

    bibhratī
    holding up

    her form
    uttamam
    most beautiful.
    Once that prostitute, desiring to bring a lover into her house, stood outside in the doorway at night showing her beautiful form.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Once, that prostitute, desiring to bring a lover into her house, stood outside in the doorway at night showing her beautiful form. Saṅkete means “in the place for enjoyment.” She stood there to attract someone.
    Personal Guidance
    Like the svairiṇī waiting at her door for a lover who never comes, the mind that hangs on viṣaya keeps grand appointments with disappointment.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice one viṣaya you are secretly 'dressing up' for today — a person, a purchase, an outcome — and refuse to step out to the doorway for it.
    • Each time the mind rehearses an imagined meeting with some object of enjoyment, redirect that same anticipatory energy into one act of bhakti — a round of japa, a śloka, a service.
    • Before sleep, name the 'lover at the door' your mind kept waiting for today, and offer that waiting itself to Kṛṣṇa as a confession, not as a fantasy.
    Reflection Questions
    • What specific enjoyment am I standing 'in the doorway' for, beautifully arranged, even though I know in honest moments it will not come or will not satisfy?
    • Where in my sādhana am I behaving like the svairiṇī — externally adorned for Kṛṣṇa, but inwardly waiting for a different visitor?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before you post the next photo or send the next message, ask honestly whose 'arrival' — a like, a reply, a particular person — you are really standing in the doorway for, and consider not posting it at all today.
    • When you catch yourself rehearsing an imagined conversation with a crush, a recruiter, or a friend group, pause and chant one round of japa before continuing to dress up that scene in your mind.
    • Refuse the campus story that says 'I am only fully alive when someone is coming for me' — spend one evening this week deliberately alone with a śloka and notice what your mind does at the doorway.
    householder
    • Notice the small daily 'doorways' — checking the phone for a client, waiting for a spouse's mood to shift, anticipating a child's grade — and bring at least one of them under sādhana rather than under hope.
    • When a long-anticipated family or career meeting does not go as you arranged, treat the disappointment as the Bhāgavatam's own teaching arriving on time, and discuss it that evening with your spouse instead of brooding alone.
    • Refuse to organize the household entirely around viṣaya-appointments — protect a fixed time for kīrtana and śravaṇa that does not move for any 'lover at the door,' however legitimate.
    senior
    • Identify one long-standing 'doorway' you are still dressing up for — a recognition, a reconciliation, a visit from someone — and consciously hand that waiting over to Kṛṣṇa rather than to time.
    • When you mentor a younger devotee or family member, share honestly one svairiṇī-pattern from your own life so they learn to recognize it earlier than you did, without dramatizing it.
    • Use the loosening of bodily attractions in this stage not as loss but as the natural emptying of the doorway, and let each renounced appointment become extra time for harināma and remembrance of departure.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading this verse as being about 'other people's lust' rather than recognizing one's own mind standing at the door of the senses.
    • Mistaking the svairiṇī's eventual turning toward the Lord as permission to keep flirting with viṣaya 'a little longer' before one finally surrenders.
    • Treating outward renunciation — plain dress, withdrawn manner — as proof of inner detachment, while the mind quietly keeps its own saṅketa appointments.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one 'beautiful form' you have been presenting to the world this past week — to whom were you really showing it, and what were you hoping would arrive?
    • Where does our group draw the line between healthy desire within varṇāśrama and the svairiṇī-mind that keeps waiting at the door of viṣaya?
    • If anticipation itself is the trap in this verse, what does our group think honest sādhana looks like when the 'lover' simply does not come?
    Role Play

    One student plays a sincere devotee who has spent the evening polishing their appearance, rehearsing what to say, and waiting for a particular person's message or visit that never arrives; another student plays a senior Vaiṣṇava friend who has noticed this pattern and gently, without shaming, helps them see the svairiṇī standing in the doorway of their own mind and redirect that same readiness toward Kṛṣṇa before the night is out.

    Objections & Responses
    • This verse is degrading — why does śāstra dwell on a prostitute at all, and what could a serious modern student possibly gain from it?
      Śāstra is not gossiping about her; it is holding up a mirror. The Bhāgavatam uses her precisely because every conditioned mind, however respectable on the outside, stands in some doorway waiting for a viṣaya-lover. Refusing the mirror because the image embarrasses us is exactly the impersonalist evasion the Gauḍīya line warns against — we are asked to see ourselves in her, and then, like her, eventually to turn toward the Lord.
    • Desire is natural and even healthy; treating ordinary attraction as the svairiṇī's waiting at the door sounds like religious shame dressed up as philosophy.
      Gauḍīya teachers do not condemn desire as such — desire directed to Kṛṣṇa is the very substance of bhakti. What this verse exposes is not attraction but the mind's habit of staking its happiness on a meeting that depends on viṣaya arriving on cue. Real vairāgya is not the absence of feeling; it is no longer needing the doorway, because the heart has found a Lover who actually comes.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.24

    ↗
    मार्ग आगच्छतो वीक्ष्य पुरुषान् पुरुषर्षभ । तान् शुल्कदान् वित्तवत: कान्तान् मेनेऽर्थकामुकी ॥ २४ ॥

    O best among men, this prostitute was very anxious to get money, and as she stood on the street at night she studied all the men who were passing by, thinking, “Oh, this one surely has money. I know he can pay the price, and I am sure he would enjoy my company very much.” Thus she thought about all the men on the street.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    O best among men! This prostitute was very anxious to get money, and as she stood on the street at night she studied all the men who were passing by, thinking, “Oh, this one surely has money. I know he can pay the price.” Paying the price the men would enjoy sex.
    Personal Guidance
    The prostitute sized up passersby purely as sources of wealth — a mirror for the calculating, transactional eye we turn on others when consumed by artha-kāmukī.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice the next time you scan a room and silently rank people by what they can give you — and quietly drop the calculation.
    • Before opening a chat or email, ask whether you are approaching this person as a soul or as a wallet, and adjust your tone accordingly.
    • Refuse to flatter or perform warmth toward someone today simply because they have money, status, or influence you want.
    Reflection Questions
    • Whose face lights up in my mind chiefly because of what they can pay me, promote me to, or do for me — and what does that reveal about my own heart?
    • Where in my so-called service or friendship am I quietly standing on the street at night, studying passersby for their śulka?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before you message someone on campus or in a group chat, notice whether you are reaching out because you like them or because they have notes, connections, or clout you want.
    • Refuse, for one week, to curate your social media around what will get likes or sponsorships, and watch what your posting becomes when the śulka is removed.
    • Pick one friendship that has quietly become transactional — favors, rides, homework — and rebuild it on something other than usefulness.
    householder
    • At the next family decision about schools, jobs, or marriage matches, name out loud where artha-kāmukī is steering the discussion and where genuine welfare is.
    • Speak to a colleague or client today whom you usually rush past because they cannot advance your career, and give them your full attention.
    • Review your hospitality this month — who got invited home, who got the better seat — and ask honestly whether wealth or position decided it.
    senior
    • Notice which children, grandchildren, or juniors you favor with time and counsel — and whether their material standing is quietly shaping that allotment.
    • Begin loosening the habit of valuing your own days by what they still produce or earn, and let service to Kṛṣṇa replace artha as the measure.
    • Mentor at least one younger person whom you would never have approached in your earning years because they had nothing to offer you.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading this verse as being about other people — moneylenders, salesmen, influencers — and never about one's own subtle transactional gaze.
    • Mistaking outward renunciation of money for freedom from artha-kāmukī while still measuring relationships by usefulness.
    • Condemning the prostitute morally and missing that she is being shown as an honest portrait of the conditioned soul's own calculus.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one relationship in your life where you have caught yourself appraising the other person's wealth or utility — and what shifted when you saw it.
    • Is there a meaningful difference between a prostitute on the street and a devotee who cultivates wealthy donors? Argue both sides honestly.
    • When is it legitimate to consider someone's resources (hiring, marriage, partnership) and when does that cross into artha-kāmukī?
    Role Play

    Two students pair up. One plays a temple fundraiser meeting a prospective wealthy donor for chai; the other plays the donor. Run the conversation twice — first with the fundraiser inwardly sizing the donor up as a śulka-dātā, then again seeing him first as a soul. Afterward, both report what changed in tone, eye contact, and what was actually said.

    Objections & Responses
    • This verse just shames a woman doing what she had to do to survive — why dwell on her at all?
      The verse is not a moral verdict on her livelihood; Śukadeva uses her as a precise diagnostic image of the mind that values others by what they can pay. The teaching turns the lamp on us, not on her, and in fact she will later become Piṅgalā the renunciate — the very person who sees through this calculation.
    • Evaluating people by what they can offer is just basic prudence — networking, hiring, even choosing a spouse all require it.
      Prudent assessment of capacity is not the issue; artha-kāmukī is when that assessment becomes the primary lens, displacing the recognition of the person as an eternal servant of Kṛṣṇa. The dhīra can weigh resources without his heart being colonized by them.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.25-26

    ↗
    आगतेष्वपयातेषु सा सङ्केतोपजीविनी । अप्यन्यो वित्तवान् कोऽपि मामुपैष्यति भूरिद: ॥ २५ ॥ एवं दुराशया ध्वस्तनिद्रा द्वार्यवलम्बती । निर्गच्छन्ती प्रविशती निशीथं समपद्यत ॥ २६ ॥

    As the prostitute Piṅgalā stood in the doorway, many men came and went, walking by her house. Her only means of sustenance was prostitution, and therefore she anxiously thought, “Maybe this one who is coming now is very rich… Oh, he is not stopping, but I am sure someone else will come. Surely this man who is coming now will want to pay me for my love, and he will probably give lots of money.” Thus, with vain hope, she remained leaning against the doorway, unable to finish her business and go to sleep. Out of anxiety she would sometimes walk out toward the street, and sometimes she went back into her house. In this way, the midnight hour gradually arrived.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    As many men passed by her house, Piṅgalā, who lived by prostitution, thought, “Maybe this rich one will come and give me lots of money.” Thus, with vain hope, she remained leaning against the doorway and could not go to sleep. Out of anxiety she would sometimes walk out in the street and sometimes she went back into her house. In this way, midnight arrived. Midnight (niśītham) arrived.
    Personal Guidance
    Pingala's restless vigil at the doorway shows how vain hope of material reward keeps the soul awake, anxious, and unable to rest in Krishna.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice the moment today when you keep refreshing a screen, inbox, or notification, hoping the next arrival will be the one that pays off, and consciously step away.
    • Name aloud the specific person, deal, or outcome you are leaning in the doorway waiting for, and offer that waiting itself to Krishna before sleeping tonight.
    • Refuse to extend your working day past its natural close just because one more transaction might come; treat the closing of the day as a small surrender.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which doorway am I still leaning against, telling myself the next passerby will finally make the wait worthwhile, and what is that vigil costing my inner life?
    • Where have I confused anxious hope with genuine effort, and dressed up restlessness as diligence?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Notice the next time you keep checking likes, replies, or grades after submitting, and close the app for the rest of the evening instead of leaning in that doorway.
    • Name the specific friend group, romantic interest, or admission result you are hoping will finally validate you, and tell one trusted person you are trying to loosen that grip.
    • Refuse to stay up past your decided bedtime scrolling for one more interesting thing; treat sleeping on time as a small act of surrender.
    householder
    • Set a firm hour at which you stop checking work messages tonight, even if a big client might still write, and let that boundary be your offering.
    • Have an honest conversation with your spouse about which financial outcome you are silently leaning in the doorway for, and whether the family's peace is being mortgaged to that wait.
    • Refuse to teach your children, by example, that the next promotion or purchase is the one that will finally make the household happy; let them see you close the day in contentment.
    senior
    • Identify the recognition, inheritance arrangement, or reconciliation you are still waiting for from family or former colleagues, and begin releasing the expectation in your evening prayers.
    • Refuse to measure your remaining years by what may still arrive at your door; instead, account each day by what you have offered to Krishna and to those you mentor.
    • Use the wakeful hours of old age, when sleep often thins, not to rehearse old disappointments but to turn the mind toward the holy name, as Pingala finally did.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Reading the verse as a condemnation of others (sex workers, salespeople, hustlers) rather than recognizing the same calculating mind in oneself.
    • Mistaking the lesson as a call to stop working or earning, when the real target is the inner posture of vain expectation, not the outer activity.
    • Romanticizing Pingala's exhaustion as if suffering itself were purifying, missing that it is her turning toward Krishna, not the misery, that liberates her.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one concrete doorway you have leaned against this week, refreshing and waiting, and let the group push back on whether the wait was really necessary.
    • Pingala's profession is named bluntly in the verse; discuss whether your own livelihood has any structural feature that keeps you in a similar posture of vain hope, and what honest reform would look like.
    • Debate: is it possible to run a business, a career, or a family life without ever leaning in the doorway, or is some degree of expectant waiting unavoidable for a householder?
    Role Play

    One student plays Pingala at midnight, exhausted at the doorway, still scanning the street for a wealthy patron. Another student plays a sober friend who has just walked up and noticed her there. The friend must, without preaching, help Pingala see the pattern she is caught in and gently turn her attention toward a more reliable shelter. The rest of the group observes and afterward names the moments where the friend slipped into either flattery or moralizing.

    Objections & Responses
    • This verse is about a prostitute in ancient India; it has nothing to say to a modern professional with a respectable job.
      The outer occupation is incidental; the verse diagnoses the inner economy of the conditioned soul, who stations herself at the doorway of the senses and waits for the next arrival to finally satisfy her. Anyone who has ever refreshed an inbox at midnight hoping for the email that will change everything is standing exactly where Pingala stood.
    • Hope is a virtue. Telling people to give up hope of reward sounds like a recipe for depression and passivity.
      The verse does not attack hope as such; it attacks misplaced hope in a source that cannot deliver. Krishna is not asking you to stop hoping, but to redirect your hope toward the one shelter that does not pass by your door and leave you waiting. That redirection is the beginning of real rest, not depression.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.27

    ↗
    तस्या वित्ताशया शुष्यद्वक्त्राया दीनचेतस: । निर्वेद: परमो जज्ञे चिन्ताहेतु: सुखावह: ॥ २७ ॥
    tasyā vittāśayā śuṣyad-
    [tasyaa vittaashayaa shushyad-]
    vaktrāyā dīna-cetasaḥ
    [vaktraayaa deena-chetasah]
    nirvedaḥ paramo jajṣe
    [nirvedah paramo jajshe]
    cintā-hetuḥ sukhāvahaḥ
    [chintaa-hetuh sukhaavahah]
    Word-for-word
    tasyāḥ — of her vitta — for money āśayā — by the desire śuṣyat — dried up vaktrāyāḥ — her face dīna — morose cetasaḥ — her mind nirvedaḥ — detachment paramaḥ — very great jajṣe — awakened cintā — anxiety hetuḥ — because of sukha — happiness āvahaḥ — bringing.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    bringing.
    As the night wore on, the prostitute, who intensely desired money, gradually became morose, and her face dried up. Thus being filled with anxiety for money and most disappointed, she began to feel a great detachment from her situation, and happiness arose in her mind.
    Purport

    It appears from these verses that on this particular night the prostitute Piṅgalā was not at all successful in attracting customers to her house. Being completely frustrated and disappointed, she gradually became indifferent to her situation. Thus, great suffering sometimes leads one to the path of enlightenment; or, according to a Sanskrit proverb, disappointment gives rise to the greatest satisfaction.

    The prostitute had dedicated her life to satisfying the lusty desires of many men. Engaging her mind, body and words in the service of paying lovers, she completely forgot the devotional service of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, and thus her mind was most unsteady and disturbed. Finally, being completely frustrated, her face and throat drying up, she began to feel indifferent to her situation, and happiness arose in her mind.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The prostitute, who intensely desired money, gradually became morose and her face dried up. Because of that worry for money, she began to feel a great detachment from her situation and happiness arose in her mind. Worrying about money was the cause of her detachment.
    Personal Guidance
    When the chase for sense-pleasure and money finally exhausts you, that very disappointment can ripen into nirveda — the doorway to Kṛṣṇa.
    Practical Applications
    • Name one viṣaya you keep chasing despite repeated disappointment, and today refuse it for one full evening.
    • When frustration arises over money or pleasure not arriving, pause and ask whether this exhaustion is Kṛṣṇa's mercy calling you inward.
    • Replace one hour usually spent on restless craving with japa or hearing about Kṛṣṇa, letting the dryness become an offering.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my life has repeated disappointment quietly been preparing me for vairāgya, and have I been numbing it instead of receiving it?
    • Which "customers" — people, habits, ambitions — am I still hoping will arrive tonight to fill the emptiness only Kṛṣṇa can fill?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Notice the hollow feeling after scrolling social media late at night and treat that dryness as a small Piṅgalā moment — close the app and chant a round instead of reaching for the next reel.
    • When a crush, friend group, or exam result disappoints you, refuse the urge to immediately replace it with another hope, and sit with the emptiness long enough to ask what it is really pointing toward.
    • Pick one ambition you have been chasing mostly for validation — a follower count, a brand-name college, a particular relationship — and honestly ask Kṛṣṇa in your morning prayer whether He wants you to keep chasing it.
    householder
    • When a deal falls through, a bonus is denied, or a family plan collapses, pause before complaining to your spouse and silently thank Kṛṣṇa for tightening the rope on your viṣaya-attachment.
    • Audit one recurring expense or pursuit that is driven by status rather than need, and redirect that money or time this month toward devotional service or the spiritual education of your children.
    • Speak openly with your spouse about a disappointment you are both carrying, and reframe it together as Kṛṣṇa's arrangement to deepen your shared sādhana rather than as a problem to be fixed by more earning.
    senior
    • Sit with the specific disappointments of this stage — a body that no longer obeys, ambitions that did not ripen, relationships that did not deliver — and let each one become a deliberate act of releasing viṣaya at Kṛṣṇa's feet.
    • Mentor a younger person not by telling them to succeed, but by honestly describing one prize you chased that turned out to be Piṅgalā's empty night.
    • Use the natural drying-up of late life as Piṅgalā used hers: simplify possessions, shorten the guest list of desires, and lengthen the time given each day to hearing and chanting about Kṛṣṇa.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Mistaking ordinary depression or burnout for genuine nirveda, and using the verse to spiritualize self-pity rather than turn to Kṛṣṇa.
    • Waiting passively for life to grind you down instead of actively withdrawing from viṣaya through sādhana now.
    • Romanticizing Piṅgalā's situation while still secretly hoping the next paycheck, relationship, or success will finally satisfy.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share a moment when a disappointment in money, status, or pleasure actually felt like mercy in retrospect — and one where it only produced bitterness. What made the difference?
    • Is it honest to call our modern dissatisfaction nirveda, or is it usually just rajas-tamas exhaustion dressed up as detachment? Press one another.
    • If happiness arose in Piṅgalā's mind only when her face dried up, what does that say about the kind of comfort we are currently arranging for ourselves?
    Role Play

    One student plays a friend who has just been passed over for a long-awaited promotion and is venting bitterly about wasted years; the other, as a fellow practitioner, must neither offer worldly consolation nor preach abstractly, but help the friend see whether this dry-mouthed disappointment might be the same mercy that came to Piṅgalā.

    Objections & Responses
    • This verse glorifies failure and despair — it sounds like the tradition is telling poor and disappointed people to just give up and call it spirituality.
      Kṛṣṇa is not glorifying despair; He is showing through Piṅgalā that the soul is structurally incapable of satisfaction in viṣaya, and that recognizing this is intelligence, not resignation. The verse does not ask the successful to fail, but asks the disappointed not to waste their disappointment by chasing the next illusion.
    • Why wait for misery to wake us up? A healthy person should be able to pursue Kṛṣṇa without first being broken by life.
      True, and that is the whole point of sādhana — to develop vairāgya by hearing and chanting before life forces it on us. Piṅgalā's story is offered precisely so that we can learn from her dry mouth without first having to live through her night.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.28

    ↗
    तस्या निर्विण्णचित्ताया गीतं श‍ृणु यथा मम । निर्वेद आशापाशानां पुरुषस्य यथा ह्यसि: ॥ २८ ॥
    tasyā nirviṇṇa-cittāyā

    gītaṁ śṛṇu yathā mama

    [nirveda aashaa-paashaanaam]
    puruṣasya yathā hy asiḥ
    [purushasya yathaa hy asih]
    Word-for-word
    tasyāḥ — of her nirviṇṇa — disgusted cittāyāḥ — whose mind gītam — song śṛṇu — please hear yathā — as it is mama — from me nirvedaḥ — detachment āśā — of hopes and yearnings pāśānām — of the binding network puruṣasya — of a person yathā — just as hi — certainly asiḥ — the sword.

    tasyā nirviṇṇa-cittāyā

    The prostitute felt disgusted with her material situation and thus became indifferent to it. Indeed, detachment acts like a sword, cutting to pieces the binding network of material hopes and desires. Now please hear from me the song sung by the prostitute in that situation.
    Purport

    The network of material desires is created when one falsely thinks that he can establish himself permanently in the material world. This binding network should be cut by the sword of detachment; otherwise one will be forced to wander in the illusory network of māyā with no understanding of liberated life on the spiritual platform.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Detachment acts like a sword, cutting to pieces the bondage caused by aspirations. Now please hear from me exactly the song sung by the disgusted prostitute. Śṛnu yathā mama means “hear from me as it is.” Detachment is the sword for cutting the ropes produced by hopes for material enjoyment.
    Related lectures
    • VedavyasaPSW SB 11-08-28-29 - Internally active externally tolerant - 2009-05-17
      His Holiness Vedavyasa Priya Swami· 2009-05-17· English
    Personal Guidance
    Disgust with material entanglement, ripened into nirveda, becomes a sword that severs the binding net of hopes tying us to māyā.
    Practical Applications
    • Name one specific material hope you keep feeding today and consciously withdraw the next thought you give it.
    • When a familiar craving rises, treat the disappointment that follows as a teacher, not as a failure to escape.
    • Spend ten minutes tonight reviewing the desires you chased this week and ask which of them actually delivered what they promised.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which material hope am I still polishing even though it has repeatedly disappointed me, and what keeps me from cutting it?
    • Where in my life is nirveda already arising as a quiet signal, and how am I numbing it instead of letting it sharpen into a sword?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Pick one social media feed that leaves you emptier than before opening it and uninstall or mute it tonight as a small first cut.
    • After the next exam result or friend-group disappointment, write down what you actually hoped that outcome would give you, and notice the gap.
    • When peers chase a status you secretly know will not satisfy, practice quiet refusal instead of arguing, and offer that restraint to Kṛṣṇa.
    householder
    • Identify one family ambition you are pushing largely from habit, and discuss with your spouse whether it still serves anyone's real welfare.
    • Before the next major purchase, sit with the hope attached to it for a full day and ask whether the hope or the object is what you actually want.
    • Use one recurring frustration at work this week as a deliberate occasion for nirveda rather than venting, and end the day chanting in that mood.
    senior
    • Walk through your home and name three possessions you are still hoping will mean something to your children, then loosen your grip on at least one.
    • When the body's decline brings disgust with material plans, receive it as the sword the verse describes and turn that freed attention toward harināma.
    • Mentor a younger seeker by sharing one specific hope you spent decades on and what its collapse finally taught you about real shelter.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Mistaking depression or burnout for genuine nirveda and using spiritual language to dress up worldly exhaustion.
    • Cutting outer attachments while secretly preserving the inner hope that the material world will eventually cooperate.
    • Treating disgust as the goal rather than as the blade that clears the ground for surrender to Kṛṣṇa.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one hope you have privately given up on, and discuss whether that surrender felt closer to despair or to nirveda.
    • Where do you disagree with the idea that disgust with the material can be spiritually healthy rather than merely negative?
    • When has a disappointment functioned in your life like the sword described here, and what did it actually cut?
    Role Play

    A friend has just lost a long-pursued promotion and tells you they are done chasing career success and want to live simply from now on. Play the conversation twice: once where you affirm whatever they say, and once where you help them test whether this is nirveda ripening or wounded pride speaking, using the verse's image of the sword.

    Objections & Responses
    • Calling detachment a sword sounds harsh and life-denying; healthy people pursue hopes, they do not amputate them.
      The verse does not attack hope itself but the binding network of hopes that promise permanence where none exists; the sword cuts the illusion, not the heart, and frees real desire to flow toward Kṛṣṇa where it can finally be satisfied.
    • If a prostitute's disgust can be spiritually instructive, are you romanticizing suffering and telling people to wait for life to break them?
      Prabhupāda is not prescribing degradation; he is pointing out that when honest reflection on material results yields nirveda, that disgust is precious and should be used as a blade rather than smothered with fresh distractions.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.29

    ↗
    न ह्यङ्गाजातनिर्वेदो देहबन्धं जिहासति । यथा विज्ञानरहितो मनुजो ममतां नृप ॥ २९ ॥
    na hy aṅgājāta-nirvedo
    [na hy angaajaata-nirvedo]
    deha-bandhaṁ jihāsati
    [deha-bandham jihaasati]
    yathā vijṣāna-rahito
