    there is no doubt about it.
    I am now completely satisfied, and I have full faith in the Lord’s mercy. Therefore I will maintain myself with whatever comes of its own accord. I shall enjoy life with only the Lord, because He is the real source of love and happiness.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Satisfied, having faith in the Lord and maintaining myself with whatever comes of its own accord, I shall enjoy life with only the Lord, because he is the real source of love and happiness. What does she desire to do after surrendering? That is explained. What does she has faith in? She has faith that she will enjoy with the Lord.
    Personal Guidance
    Be satisfied with whatever comes of its own accord, trust Kṛṣṇa's mercy, and find real love and happiness in Him alone.
    Practical Applications
    • Today, refuse one impulse to acquire or arrange more than what arrives on its own — eat, spend, or accept only yathā-lābha.
    • Each time anxiety about provision rises, audibly remind yourself that Kṛṣṇa is the source, and turn that worry into a moment of remembrance.
    • Spend at least ten minutes alone with Kṛṣṇa today — japa, reading, or quiet prayer — and let Him, not company or entertainment, be your enjoyment.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where in my life am I still arranging and scheming for more, secretly disbelieving that what comes of its own accord is enough?
    • When I am alone with Kṛṣṇa, do I actually taste Him as ramaṇa — the real lover and source of happiness — or do I reach for substitutes?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • This week, unfollow or mute three accounts that fuel comparison about what classmates or peers are acquiring, and notice how much of your striving was downstream of that feed.
    • When a friend group plans yet another outing you cannot really afford, practice naming yathā-lābha out loud to yourself and choose contentment over keeping up.
    • Pick one evening alone, without phone or scrolling, and treat it as time with Kṛṣṇa — japa, reading, or prayer — to learn the taste of Him as ramaṇa before you are older.
    householder
    • Sit with your spouse and identify one ongoing financial or lifestyle upgrade you have been chasing, and honestly ask whether it is need or restlessness; agree together to let it go for now.
    • When tempted to load expectations of happiness onto your spouse or children, silently remember that Kṛṣṇa is the real ramaṇa, and let your family love be lighter and less demanding.
    • Model yathā-lābha for your children by visibly receiving what comes — meals, gifts, setbacks — with audible gratitude to Kṛṣṇa rather than comparison or complaint.
    senior
    • Take inventory of possessions, savings, and arrangements that you have been holding 'in case,' and begin giving away or simplifying what exceeds yathā-lābha for this stage of life.
    • When the body's diminishments and the loss of companions sharpen loneliness, deliberately turn that ache toward Kṛṣṇa as ramaṇa, rather than toward television, news, or grievance.
    • In mentoring younger devotees and family members, speak frankly about how little of what you chased actually delivered, and witness to Kṛṣṇa as the only source of love and happiness that did not fade.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Mistaking passive laziness or fatalism for yathā-lābha satisfaction, and using the verse to excuse neglect of duty.
    • Performing outward contentment while inwardly resenting what has come and envying what others have received.
    • Treating Kṛṣṇa as a backup source of comfort when relationships fail, rather than as the primary ramaṇa from the start.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one area where 'whatever comes of its own accord' would genuinely feel like deprivation to you — and examine why.
    • Is it possible to be satisfied with yathā-lābha in a modern economy that demands constant striving, or does this verse require withdrawal? Disagree honestly.
    • What is the difference between needing Kṛṣṇa because no human love is available and choosing Kṛṣṇa as ramaṇa even when human company is plentiful?
    Role Play

    Two students pair up. One plays a friend who has just received a major promotion and is urging the other to chase a similar opportunity; the other plays a practitioner trying to articulate, without sounding pious or evasive, why they are choosing to remain with what has come of its own accord and to keep Kṛṣṇa as the center of their enjoyment.

    Objections & Responses
    • Being satisfied with whatever comes is a recipe for poverty and exploitation — the world will simply take advantage of someone who refuses to fight for more.
      The verse is not a prohibition on responsible action within one's sva-dharma; it is a prohibition on the inner grasping that turns every gain into a new hunger. A householder still works, earns, and protects dependents, but the heart rests in Kṛṣṇa's arrangement rather than in the scheming mind. Exploitation is resisted from a place of steadiness, not from a place of fear.
    • Saying Kṛṣṇa is the real source of love sounds like a way to avoid the hard work of human relationships and intimacy.
      Gauḍīya teaching does not abolish human relationships; it reorders them. When Kṛṣṇa is ramaṇa at the center, one's love for spouse, children, and friends becomes steadier and less demanding, because it is no longer compensating for an absence the soul is trying to fill. The verse asks us to stop placing on others the burden of being our ultimate source of happiness — which is the very weight that breaks most human bonds.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.41

    ↗
    संसारकूपे पतितं विषयैर्मुषितेक्षणम् । ग्रस्तं कालाहिनात्मानं कोऽन्यस्त्रातुमधीश्वर: ॥ ४१ ॥
    saṁsāra-kūpe patitaṁ
    [samsaara-koope patitam]
    viṣayair muṣitekṣaṇam
    [vishayair mushitekshanam]
    grastaṁ kālāhinātmānaṁ
    [grastam kaalaahinaatmaanam]
    ko ’nyas trātum adhīśvaraḥ
    [ko ’nyas traatum adheeshvarah]
    Word-for-word
    saṁsāra — material existence kūpe — in the dark well patitam — fallen viṣayaiḥ — by sense gratification muṣita — stolen away īkṣaṇam — vision grastam — seized kāla — of time ahinā — by the serpent ātmānam — the living entity kaḥ — who anyaḥ — else trātum — is capable of delivering adhīśvaraḥ — the Supreme Personality of Godhead.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    saṁsāra-kūpe patitaṁ
    saṁsāra
    material existence
    kūpe
    in the dark well
    patitam
    fallen
    viṣayair muṣitekṣaṇam
    viṣayaiḥ
    by sense gratification

    ko ’nyas trātum adhīśvaraḥ
    trātum
    is capable of delivering
    adhīśvaraḥ
    the Supreme Personality of Godhead.
    The intelligence of the living entity is stolen away by activities of sense gratification, and thus he falls into the dark well of material existence. Within that well he is then seized by the deadly serpent of time. Who else but the Supreme Personality of Godhead could save the poor living entity from such a hopeless condition?
    Purport

    Piṅgalā stated in a previous verse that even the demigods are unable to give actual happiness to a woman. One may question who has authorized this lady to reject even such exalted personalities as Brahmā, Śiva and the other demigods. The answer given here is that if one actually wants to solve all the problems of life and go back home, back to Godhead, then the only solution is to take shelter of the lotus feet of the Lord. It is well known that the demigods themselves are subject to birth and death. As stated by Lord Śiva himself, mukti-pradātā sarveṣāṁ viṣṇur eva na saṁśayaḥ: “There is no doubt that Viṣṇu is the deliverer of liberation for everyone.”

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Who else but the Supreme Lord can save the living entity who has fallen into the well of saṁsāra, has become blinded by sense objects and has been devoured by the snake of time? “Why are you so eager to give up worshipping the devatās for Viṣṇu?” This verse answers.
    Related lectures
    • Akuti Mataji SB 11-08-41-44 - 2017-05-17 ISKCON Alachua
      Her Grace Akuti Mataji· 2017-05-17· English
    Personal Guidance
    Sense gratification blinds us, time devours us, and we lie helpless at the bottom of saṁsāra's well — only Kṛṣṇa can lift us out.
    Practical Applications
    • Name the one viṣaya that most reliably steals your intelligence this week — food, screen, ambition, fantasy — and refuse it once today as a deliberate offering to Kṛṣṇa.
    • Each morning, before reaching for the phone, speak aloud: 'I am in the well; Kṛṣṇa alone is my adhīśvara,' and let that frame the day's first hour.
    • When the fear of aging, illness, or wasted years rises, do not bargain with demigods of career or health — turn directly to Kṛṣṇa's name and chant for ten minutes.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which sense-object has so quietly 'stolen my eyes' that I no longer even notice I have fallen into the well — and what would it cost me to see again?
    • When the serpent of kāla tightens around something I love, do I still run to lesser shelters first, and what does that habit reveal about my actual faith in Kṛṣṇa as adhīśvara?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Pick the one app or feed that most reliably 'steals your eyes' before exams or sleep, and uninstall or time-lock it today — name it aloud as the viṣaya it is.
    • When peer pressure pushes you toward a relationship, party, or identity that promises rescue from loneliness, pause and ask: is this a hand from outside the well, or another wall inside it?
    • Choose one daily anchor — sixteen minutes of japa, a Bhāgavatam verse, or a morning maṅgala-ārati audio — and hold it even when friends, deadlines, and FOMO say you cannot spare the time.
    householder
    • Before the next big family decision — school, house, job change — sit quietly and check whether you are seeking Kṛṣṇa's shelter or merely a better arrangement of the well's furniture.
    • Audit one recurring expense or commitment driven by viṣaya (status food, status schooling, status vacations) and redirect part of that money or time toward devotional service this month.
    • Speak with your spouse this week about the serpent of kāla — aging parents, your own mortality, the children's eventual departure — and agree on one concrete sādhana you will protect together.
    senior
    • Take inventory of the shelters you have leaned on across decades — career, health, reputation, even family — and write down which of them the serpent of kāla has already begun to swallow.
    • Mentor one younger person this month not by giving worldly advice but by quietly showing them what it looks like to take Kṛṣṇa as adhīśvara when the body is no longer cooperating.
    • Each night, before sleep, rehearse leaving the body with Kṛṣṇa's name on the tongue — treat sleep as a small dress rehearsal for the final exit from the well.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Treating the well of saṁsāra as a place to be redecorated rather than escaped — using bhakti to make material life smoother instead of to get out.
    • Approaching demigods, gurus, therapists, or self-help systems as the actual rescuers, with Kṛṣṇa kept as a vague backup who endorses our other shelters.
    • Hearing 'who else but Kṛṣṇa can save' as poetic exaggeration rather than a literal diagnosis of our hopelessness without Him.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share with your partner: what is the specific viṣaya that most often 'muṣita-īkṣaṇam' — steals your seeing — and how do you know it has happened only in hindsight?
    • Piṅgalā rejected even Brahmā and Śiva as ultimate shelters. Where in your life are you still holding out hope that a high-status human, institution, or system will finally rescue you?
    • Discuss honestly: is the image of saṁsāra as a dark well with a serpent of time too dramatic for modern life, or is its drama exactly what we have learned to numb ourselves to?
    Role Play

    One student plays a successful 45-year-old who has just received a serious medical diagnosis and is frantically researching specialists, supplements, and second opinions. The other plays a Vaiṣṇava friend who must, without dismissing the medical effort, gently apply this verse — naming the well, the serpent of kāla, and the one adhīśvara — in language the patient can actually hear. Swap roles and try again with a different scenario: a parent whose child has drifted into addiction.

    Objections & Responses
    • Calling material life a 'dark well' is depressive and life-denying. My life has real joys — family, work, beauty — and this verse seems to dismiss all of it.
      The verse does not deny that the well has flickers of pleasure; it denies that those flickers add up to rescue. The serpent of kāla swallows every joy you have ever had, including the ones you are defending right now. Kṛṣṇa-bhakti does not erase love of family, work, or beauty — it relocates them, so that the same affections become rungs of the ladder out instead of decorations inside the well.
    • Why only Kṛṣṇa? Surely sincere people in other traditions, or even good therapy and ethical living, can lift a person out of suffering without invoking a specific Personality of Godhead.
      We are not saying other shelters give zero relief — we are saying they cannot deliver from birth, death, and time, which is what this verse is actually about. Lord Śiva himself declares mukti-pradātā sarveṣāṁ viṣṇur eva na saṁśayaḥ. The personalism is not sectarianism; it is precision. A well needs someone outside the well with hands, not a philosophy circulating inside it.
    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.42

    ↗
    आत्मैव ह्यात्मनो गोप्ता निर्विद्येत यदाखिलात् । अप्रमत्त इदं पश्येद् ग्रस्तं कालाहिना जगत् ॥ ४२ ॥
    ātmaiva hy ātmano goptā
    [aatmaiva hy aatmano goptaa]
    nirvidyeta yadākhilāt
    [nirvidyeta yadaakhilaat]
    apramatta idaṁ paśyed
    [apramatta idam pashyed]
    grastaṁ kālāhinā jagat
    [grastam kaalaahinaa jagat]
    Word-for-word
    ātmā — the soul eva — alone hi — certainly ātmanaḥ — of himself goptā — the protector nirvidyeta — becomes detached yadā — when akhilāt — from all material things apramattaḥ — without material fever idam — this paśyet — can see grastam — seized kāla — of time ahinā — by the serpent jagat — the universe.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    ātmaiva hy ātmano goptā
    ātmā
    the soul
    eva
    alone
    nirvidyeta yadākhilāt
    hi
    certainly
    ātmanaḥ
    of himself
    goptā
    the protector
    nirvidyeta
    becomes detached
    yadā
    when
    akhilāt
    from all material things
    apramatta idaṁ paśyed
    apramattaḥ
    without material fever
    idam
    this
    paśyet
    can see
    grastaṁ kālāhinā jagat
    grastam
    seized
    kāla
    of time
    ahinā
    by the serpent
    jagat
    the universe.
    When the living entity sees that the entire universe has been seized by the serpent of time, he becomes sober and sane and at that time detaches himself from all material sense gratification. In that condition the living entity is qualified to be his own protector.
    Purport

    In this verse, Piṅgalā states that by the Lord’s mercy a self-realized soul can see that the entire universe is existing within the jaws of the great serpent of time. Certainly this is not a promising situation, and one who sees it loses his desire for sense gratification. Thus, by the Lord’s causeless mercy, the spiritually sane living entity can protect himself from illusion.

    Since Piṅgalā is now glorifying the Personality of Godhead for His mercy in awarding salvation, the following question might arise: Is she worshiping the Lord out of love, or is she merely a salvationist desiring liberation from material existence? In answer it may be said that in her Kṛṣṇa conscious situation she is already liberated, even though remaining in this world. Her program will now be simply to render loving service to the Personality of Godhead without any personal desire, including that of salvation.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    When the living entity sees that the entire universe has been seized by the serpent of time, and becoming sober, detaches himself from all material enjoyment, he is qualified to protect himself from saṁsāra. “Will you worship the Lord with the idea of getting deliverance?” No. When the jīva becomes detached from all material enjoyment (akhilāt), since he sees the world devoured by time, at that time ātmā is his own protection from saṁsāra (It is not necessary for the Lord to do this.) My ātmā has this very nature. Thus I have been delivered from saṁsāra automatically. I will then worship the Lord in prema alone.
    Personal Guidance
    Once you see the whole universe clamped in the jaws of time, sense gratification loses its grip — and only then can the self become its own protector.
    Practical Applications
    • Before reaching for a habitual indulgence today, pause and name the thing time has already taken from you to break its glamour.
    • Set a brief evening review where you ask which of today's pursuits would still matter if you knew you had one year left.
    • Refuse to outsource your spiritual protection to circumstances or people — chant, hear, and decide today as if no one else will guard your soul.
    Reflection Questions
    • Where am I still living as if time has exempted my comforts, my body, or my plans from its mouth?
    • What would change in how I spend the next twenty-four hours if I truly accepted that I alone am responsible for protecting my own consciousness?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Before opening social media tonight, sit for two minutes and notice how the feed itself is a small mouth of time eating your attention, then choose whether to feed it.
    • Pick one exam, crush, or friendship anxiety that feels enormous and ask honestly where it will sit in your life ten years from now — let that perspective decide today's effort.
    • Stop waiting for parents, teachers, or friends to rescue your inner life; commit to one daily spiritual act this week that nobody else has to remind you about.
    householder
    • Look at your calendar and bank statement this week as honest evidence of what you actually believe time will spare, and rebalance one line item toward sādhana or family kīrtana.
    • When a career or money fear hijacks you, recall that the project, the salary, and the worry are all inside the serpent's jaws — then make the next decision from that sobriety rather than panic.
    • Teach your children, by example more than words, that the adults in this house guard their own consciousness — show them you read, chant, and pray without being asked.
    senior
    • Begin loosening grip on one possession, role, or opinion you have quietly clutched for decades, and offer the release to Kṛṣṇa as preparation for the real departure.
    • Speak with one younger person this week not about your achievements but about what time has shown you cannot be kept — let your sobriety be their inheritance.
    • Treat the body's signals of aging not as insults but as the serpent's reminders, and use each one to deepen daily hearing, chanting, and remembrance rather than to grieve.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Turning the vision of kāla into morbid fatalism that paralyzes service rather than sobering one for it.
    • Using 'I am my own protector' as a license for isolated independence, ignoring guru, sādhus, and śāstra through whom the Lord's mercy actually flows.
    • Detaching from sense gratification outwardly while quietly investing the saved energy in subtler enjoyments like prestige or being seen as detached.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one area of your life where you still secretly bargain with time — and one where you have genuinely conceded its grip.
    • Piṅgalā becomes her own protector only after seeing the world swallowed by time; can someone protect themselves without first being shaken? Debate it.
    • What is the difference between fearing death and seeing the universe in the serpent's jaws? Press each other until the distinction is clear.
    Role Play

    One student plays a friend who has just received a frightening medical diagnosis and is asking whether to chase remaining pleasures or 'get serious'; the other plays a Gauḍīya practitioner trying to communicate this verse — that sobering vision of kāla is not despair but the doorway to becoming one's own protector — without sounding preachy or cold.

    Objections & Responses
    • Seeing the universe as devoured by a serpent of time sounds like depressive nihilism dressed up as religion — why would anyone choose to live in that mood?
      The verse is not asking you to feel doom; it is asking you to drop a delusion. The serpent is already there whether you look or not. Seeing it is what frees you from chasing what cannot last, and that freedom is the very ground on which loving service to Kṛṣṇa stands — sober, not sad.
    • If the soul is its own protector, why bother with guru, deity worship, or community at all?
      The verse says one becomes qualified to protect oneself precisely by the Lord's mercy, which Prabhupāda underscores comes through guru and sādhu-saṅga. Self-protection here means refusing to be carried off by the senses — not refusing the very channels through which that strength is given.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.43

    ↗
    श्रीब्राह्मण उवाच एवं व्यवसितमतिर्दुराशां कान्ततर्षजाम् । छित्त्वोपशममास्थाय शय्यामुपविवेश सा ॥ ४३ ॥

    The avadhūta said: Thus, her mind completely made up, Piṅgalā cut off all her sinful desires to enjoy sex pleasure with lovers, and she became situated in perfect peace. Then she sat down on her bed.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The avadhūta said: Thus, with determined mind, Piṅgalā, cutting off all her sinful desires arising from thirst for lovers, became situated in perfect peace and lay down on her bed.
    Personal Guidance
    Once Piṅgalā cut the durāśā for lovers' pleasure at the root, peace arrived on its own — she finally sat down on her own bed.
    Practical Applications
    • Name the one craving you keep half-feeding today, and resolve — like Piṅgalā — to cut it off rather than negotiate with it.
    • Notice the restless pacing in your own life (refreshing the phone, checking who replied) and instead sit down, the way she sat on her bed.
    • Before sleep tonight, take a single firm decision (vyavasita-mati) about a desire you will no longer chase, and offer that resolve to Kṛṣṇa.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which durāśā — a hope I myself know to be hopeless — am I still standing at the door for, and what would it cost me to finally cut it?
    • Where in my life am I mistaking exhaustion for peace, when real upaśama would only come from a settled decision like Piṅgalā's?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Pick one person, account, or fantasy you keep checking on 'just in case' something happens — make a clean decision tonight to stop, the way Piṅgalā stopped waiting at her door.
    • Before the next exam or application, separate honest effort from the durāśā of a specific outcome you have been silently rehearsing, and let only the effort remain.
    • When peer pressure pushes you toward a scene you already know leaves you empty, practice the small Piṅgalā move: physically leave, sit down somewhere quiet, and let the restlessness pass.
    householder
    • Identify one career or financial 'big break' you have been quietly waiting on for years, and discuss honestly with your spouse whether it is still a real hope or a durāśā to be cut.
    • When you catch yourself seeking from your spouse or children the kind of completion only Kṛṣṇa can give, name it inwardly and bring that longing back to your sādhana instead of pressing it on them.
    • Build a nightly five-minute 'sit down on the bed' practice — phone away, japa or quiet prayer — so the day ends in upaśama rather than in scrolling for one more hit.
    senior
    • Take inventory of the recognitions, reconciliations, or returns of affection you are still waiting for from people who will not give them, and one by one release those durāśās as Piṅgalā released hers.
    • Loosen the grip on the body's remaining pleasures — taste, comfort, being noticed — not by force but by seeing, like her, that they cannot deliver what only Kṛṣṇa can.
    • Mentor a younger seeker by sharing one durāśā you carried for decades and how cutting it finally brought peace, so they learn upaśama from your life, not just from books.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Treating Piṅgalā's renunciation as mere disgust with one bad habit, rather than as a settled vyavasita-mati about the whole pattern of seeking pleasure outside Kṛṣṇa.
    • Imagining that peace comes first and then renunciation follows — the verse shows the reverse: she cuts the false hope, and upaśama comes to her.
    • Romanticizing the outward act of 'sitting on the bed' as a posture, while still keeping the inner door open to the very desires she had just severed.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Piṅgalā was a prostitute, not a renunciant — what does it mean that her turning point is held up as instruction for us? Where does that unsettle you?
    • Share a 'durāśā' — a hope you knew was hopeless — that you kept alive anyway. What finally cut it, or what is still keeping it alive?
    • The verse links cutting desire, peace, and sitting down. In your own experience, which of those three usually comes first, and is that the right order?
    Role Play

    One student plays a friend who has spent years waiting for a particular relationship, job offer, or recognition that is clearly never coming, yet keeps 'staying ready' for it. The other student, drawing on Piṅgalā's example, has to help them name the durāśā honestly and talk through what cutting it — not just managing it — would actually look like by tonight.

    Objections & Responses
    • This sounds like repression — she just gave up on love and called it peace. Modern psychology would say she needs to process her desires, not slash them.
      Piṅgalā is not suppressing a desire she still believes in; she has seen through it. Repression keeps the hope alive underground, whereas vyavasita-mati cuts the root by recognizing the object cannot deliver what she wanted. The upaśama that follows is not numbness but the relief of no longer bargaining with an illusion.
    • Cutting off all desire seems extreme and even inhuman — surely a balanced life keeps healthy desires and only restrains the harmful ones.
      Notice the verse is specific: she cuts the durāśā, the hopeless craving aimed at kānta-tarṣa, lovers' thirst — not desire as such. In the Gauḍīya line, the goal is not zero desire but desire redirected to Kṛṣṇa. What is severed is the false hope that material enjoyment will satisfy the soul; what remains is the capacity to love, now available for its real object.
    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 11.8.44

    ↗
    आशा हि परमं दु:खं नैराश्यं परमं सुखम् । यथा सञ्छिद्य कान्ताशां सुखं सुष्वाप पिङ्गला ॥ ४४ ॥
    āśā hi paramaṁ duḥkhaṁ
    [aashaa hi paramam duhkham]
    nairāśyaṁ paramaṁ sukham
    [nairaashyam paramam sukham]
    yathā saṣchidya kāntāśāṁ
    [yathaa sashchidya kaantaashaam]
    sukhaṁ suṣvāpa piṅgalā
    [sukham sushvaapa pingalaa]
    Word-for-word
    āśā — material desire hi — certainly paramam — the greatest duḥkham — unhappiness nairāśyam — freedom from material desires paramam — the greatest sukham — happiness yathā — in that way saṣchidya — completely cutting off kānta — for lovers āśām — the desire sukham — happily suṣvāpa — she slept piṅgalā — the former prostitute, Piṅgalā.
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    Material desire is undoubtedly the cause of the greatest unhappiness, and freedom from such desire is the cause of the greatest happiness. Therefore, completely cutting off her desire to enjoy so-called lovers, Piṅgalā very happily went to sleep.
    Purport

    Thus end the purports of the humble servants of His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda to the Eleventh Canto, Eighth Chapter, of the Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam, entitled “The Story of Piṅgalā.”

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Material desire is undoubtedly the cause of the greatest unhappiness, and freedom from such desire is the cause of the greatest happiness. Therefore, completely cutting off her desire to enjoy lovers, Piṅgalā happily went to sleep. Thus ends the commentary on Eighth Chapter of the Eleventh Canto of the Bhāgavatam for the pleasure of the devotees, in accordance with the previous ācāryas. Chapter Nine The Avadhūta Concludes His Teachings 11.9: Detachment from All that Is Material 9. Detachment from All that Is Material 11.9 Summary The avadhūta brāhmaṇa describes his seven remaining gurus, beginning with the kurara bird. He also describes an additional guru, one's own body. The instruction received from the kurara bird is that attachment creates misery, but the person who is unattached and has no material possessions is qualified to achieve unlimited happiness. The avadhūta brāhmaṇa learned from the foolish, lazy child that by becoming free from anxiety a person becomes capable of worshiping the Supreme Personality of Godhead and experiencing supreme ecstasy. The instruction received from the young girl who kept just one conchshell bracelet on each wrist is that one should remain alone and thus steady one's mind. Then only will it be possible for one to fix one's mind completely on the Personality of Godhead. Once several men arrived to ask for the hand of the young girl, whose relatives had coincidentally left the house. She went inside and began to prepare food for the unexpected guests by beating rice. At that time her conchshell bracelets were making a loud noise, rattling against each other, and in order to stop this sound she broke off the bracelets one by one until at last only one remained on each arm. Just as two or more bracelets make noise, if even two people reside in the same place, what to speak of many, there is every chance of mutual quarrel and useless gossip. The avadhūta brāhmaṇa also received instruction from the arrow maker, who was so absorbed in constructing an arrow that he did not even notice that the king was passing right by him on the road. In the same way, one must strictly control one's mind, concentrating it in the worship of Lord Śrī Hari. The avadhūta brāhmaṇa learned from the serpent that a sage should wander alone, should not have any residence, should be always careful and grave, should not reveal his movements, should take assistance from no one and should speak little. The instruction obtained from the spider, who spins his web from his mouth and then withdraws it, is that the Supreme Personality of Godhead similarly creates from out of Himself the whole universe and then winds it up into Himself. From the weak insect who assumed the same form as the peśaskṛt wasp, the avadhūta brāhmaṇa learned that the living entity, under the sway of affection, hatred and fear, attains in his next life the identity of that object upon which he fixes his intelligence. Seeing that the fragile material body is subject to birth and death, one who is sober should become devoid of material attachment to this body and should properly utilize the rare gift of human life in the pursuit of knowledge, endeavoring always for the achievement of the highest goal.
    Related lectures
    • DMS SB 11-08-44 - Pingalaa Gita Part-02 - 2012-04-14 New Zealand
      His Holiness Devamrita Swami· 2012-04-14· English
    • DMS SB 11-08-44 - Pingalaa Gita Part-01 - 2012-04-13 New Zealand
      His Holiness Devamrita Swami· 2012-04-13· English
    Personal Guidance
    Material desire is the root of greatest misery; cutting it off, as Piṅgalā did, is the doorway to the deepest happiness and sleep of the soul.
    Practical Applications
    • Name one āśā — one fantasy of a future lover, gain, or recognition — that is keeping you restless, and renounce it aloud before sleeping tonight.
    • When the next pang of longing arises today, label it duḥkha instead of pleasure, and refuse to feed it with another scroll, message, or daydream.
    • Before bed, mentally lay down every outstanding hope and surrender it to Kṛṣṇa, so you sleep as Piṅgalā slept — empty and content.
    Reflection Questions
    • Which specific kāntāśā — desire for a particular person, status, or outcome — am I still nursing while telling myself it makes me happy?
    • What would actually change in my day if I believed, with Piṅgalā, that hope itself is the source of my suffering?
    For Each Age Group
    youth
    • Identify the one person whose reply or attention you keep refreshing your phone for, and decide tonight that their response is no longer the source of your happiness.
    • Before the next exam or interview, write down the outcome you are secretly clinging to, then deliberately release it so you can perform without the weight of the result.
    • Notice when peer approval feels like oxygen, and practice walking through one social situation today expecting nothing back from anyone.
    householder
    • Pick the one expectation you carry about your spouse or child — a behavior, a career path, a future — and consciously surrender it at your altar this evening.
    • When the next promotion, raise, or house upgrade rises in your mind as the thing that will finally settle you, recognize it as kāntāśā in a respectable disguise.
    • Before sleeping, hand the family's outstanding worries to Kṛṣṇa one by one so you stop carrying tomorrow's hopes into tonight's rest.
    senior
    • Make a list of the hopes you are still holding for how your children, grandchildren, or legacy will turn out, and offer them up — your remaining peace depends on it.
    • When you feel the pull to be remembered, consulted, or honored, recognize it as the same āśā in subtler clothing and let it go for the rest of the day.
    • Take Piṅgalā's resolution literally: tonight, lay your head down with nothing left to want from this world, and notice the quality of the sleep that follows.
    Common Pitfalls
    • Mistaking dull, depressed apathy for nairāśya — Piṅgalā's freedom is bright and restful, not numb.
    • Renouncing visible desires (romance, wealth) while quietly cultivating subtler ones (reputation as a renunciant, spiritual achievement).
    • Treating this verse as license to neglect prescribed duties, when the renunciation is of fruitive expectation, not of service to Kṛṣṇa.
    Discussion Prompts
    • Share one āśā you have already cut off in your life — what did the happiness on the other side actually feel like, and was it what you expected?
    • Piṅgalā was a prostitute who became a teacher of detachment in a single night; what does it say about us that we, with more facility, still cling?
    • Where do you disagree with the verse — is there a hope in your life you are unwilling to call duḥkha, and why?
    Role Play

    Two students pair up: one plays a friend who has just been ghosted by a romantic interest and is rehearsing every scenario of reconciliation; the other plays a sādhaka who must, without preaching or quoting verses, help the friend see that the hope itself is the wound, and guide them toward laying it down for one night's real sleep.

    Objections & Responses
    • Without desire, nothing gets done — civilizations, families, and even devotional service run on wanting things. This verse sounds like a recipe for paralysis.
      The verse rejects āśā for material enjoyment, not the desire to serve Kṛṣṇa. Piṅgalā did not stop living; she stopped hoping in lovers and turned that same energy toward the Lord within. Action grounded in service is tireless; action grounded in personal hope exhausts us.
    • Telling someone that hope causes their suffering sounds cruel — hope is what keeps people alive through hard times.
      Gauḍīya teachers distinguish material hope, which always disappoints because its object cannot satisfy the soul, from hope placed in Kṛṣṇa, which never fails. Piṅgalā did not become hopeless; she relocated her hope. We are not asking the sufferer to stop hoping, but to stop hoping in what cannot deliver.
    📝 My note
